Bifurcation Analysis of Generalized Hopf Bifurcation in Ordinary and Delay Differential Equations
Nathalie A. M. Delmeire, Maikel M. Bosschaert, Yuri A. Kuznetsov

Abstract
The generalized Hopf (Bautin) bifurcation is a well-studied codimension two bifurcation characterized by an equilibrium with a pair of simple purely imaginary eigenvalues as the only critical eigenvalues and the vanishing first Lyapunov coefficient. This bifurcation arises in both ordinary differential equations (ODEs) and delay differential equations (DDEs). Generically, a codimension one bifurcation curve of nonhyperbolic (double) limit cycles ( L ​ P ​ C LPC curve) emanates from a generalized Hopf point at the Hopf bifurcation curve H H . By performing the parameter-dependent center manifold reduction near this point, the first-order predictors to initiate continuation of the L ​ P ​ C LPC curve have been derived in the literature. These predictors, however, do not distinguish the curves H H and L ​ P ​ C LPC in the parameter space. In this paper, we overcome this deficiency by deriving higher-order predictors for the L ​ P ​ C LPC curve in ODEs and DDEs for the first time. The new predictors, which require seventh-order derivatives of the system, have been implemented, and their effectiveness is demonstrated in several models.



{keywords}

generalized Hopf (Bautin) bifurcation, codimension two bifurcation, higher-order predictors, continuation, Ordinary Differential Equations, Delay Differential Equations, Limit Point of Cycles, parameter-dependent center manifold

{MSCcodes}

37G05, 37G10, 34A12, 34K18

1
Introduction

Many applications use models that consist of autonomous Ordinary Differential Equations (ODEs)

\dot{x}(t)=F(x(t),\alpha),

(1)

where
x:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
F:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a smooth mapping. One is often interested in the behaviour of such systems under parameter variations. If the system has an equilibrium point, the variation of one parameter might result in a local codim 1 bifurcation. A well known example is the
(
Andronov-
)
Hopf bifurcation
. By allowing the variation of a second parameter, the first Lyapunov coefficient may vanish while the two purely imaginary eigenvalues persist. At such a point a
generalized Hopf bifurcation
(or
Bautin bifurcation
) occurs. This bifurcation, which requires two conditions to be satisfied to manifest, is a
codimension two
bifurcation. There are four other well-known local codim 2 bifurcations. For example, a Hopf-Hopf bifurcation occurs when an equilibrium has two pairs of simple purely imaginary eigenvalues. Their normal forms and local behaviour are discussed in detail in
[
15
, Chapter 8]
. In this paper, we will only deal with the generalized Hopf bifurcation. A sketch of the bifurcation diagram for the case where
l_{2}<0
is shown in
Fig.

1
. In this illustration, we can discern three different regions near the generalized Hopf bifurcation. In region
1
there is only a stable focus at the origin. If we move from region
1
to region
2
, passing the supercritical Hopf bifurcation line
H_{-}
, the equilibrium at the origin becomes unstable and a unique stable limit cycle appears. Continuing through the subcritical Hopf bifurcation line
H_{+}
into region
3
, the equilibrium recovers its stability, alongside the emergence of an unstable cycle nested within the pre-existing stable cycle. These two cycles collide and disappear at the
LPC
curve separating regions
3
and
1
, where a limit point (or fold) bifurcation of cycles occurs.

Figure 1:
Bifurcation diagram near a generalized Hopf bifurcation for the case
l_{2}<0
. Figure adapted from
[
15
]
.

In
n
-dimensional systems where a local bifurcation arises, a smooth family of parameter-dependent invariant
center manifolds

W_{\alpha}^{c}
exists for sufficiently small
\|\alpha\|
. All qualitative changes near the bifurcation point occur on this lower dimensional manifold. For the Hopf and the generalized Hopf bifurcations, this manifold is two-dimensional. If we restrict our system
Eq.

1
to the center manifold, the system can locally be transformed to a normal form. The existence of an invariant center manifold can be used to derive equations for the normal form coefficients.

Another interesting class of differential equations is that of Delay Differential Equations (DDEs). Unlike ODEs, which RHS depends only on current state information, the RHS of DDEs involves the values of the phase variables at previous times. This accounts for delays in system’s response, capturing phenomena where the evolution of a system depends not only on its current state but also on its history. A specific case of DDEs that are often encountered in applications is of the following form

\dot{x}(t)=F(x(t),x(t-\tau_{1}),\ldots,x(t-\tau_{m}),\alpha),

(2)

where
x:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
,
F:\mathbb{R}^{n\times(m+1)}\times\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a smooth mapping, and
0<\tau_{1}<\ldots<\tau_{m}=:h
are constant delays. This is also known as a
discrete DDE
.
Such systems allow for study of bifurcations, including the generalized Hopf bifurcation (i.e.
[
12
,
21
,
13
]
). In contrast to ODEs, DDEs belong to the class of infinite dimensional dynamical systems, since a natural space of initial data (state space) here is
X=C([-h,0],\mathbb{R}^{n})
. However, this state space does lead to some technical complications. This can be resolved with the help of perturbation theory for dual semigroups, which has been developed in
[
7
,
8
,
9
,
10
]
and is also known as
sun-star calculus
. Using this framework, the existence of a finite-dimensional smooth invariant center manifold has been rigorously established for DDEs
[
11
,
4
]
. On this center manifold, the infinite-dimensional system of DDEs can be reduced to a finite-dimensional system of ODEs. This in turn allows us to “lift” results from the bifurcation theory of ODEs to the theory of DDEs.

In most cases, the codim 1 bifurcation curves near a generalized Hopf bifurcation cannot be computed analytically. Instead one uses numerical methods for the location of the codim 2 bifurcation and the continuation of the emanating codim 1 bifurcation curves. If the approximate location of a generalized Hopf bifurcation point is known, we would like to switch to the
LPC
curve emanating from this point using only local information available at the generalized Hopf point. To start the continuation, we need an approximation of the
LPC
curve in the original parameter space, an approximation of the corresponding periodic orbit and the period. Combined, this forms a
predictor
. A general method to derive such approximations, using only local information at the codim 2 bifurcation point, has been introduced in
[
3
]
. Within the framework of the sun-star calculus, the normalization method developed for ODEs has been extended to DDEs
[
14
]
. These methods have already been applied to derive first-order predictors for both ODEs
[
16
]
and DDEs
[
4
]
. Such predictors, however, do not distinguish the curves
H
and
LPC
in the parameter space.

In this paper, we derive a higher-order predictor for both ODEs and DDEs that does not suffer from this drawback. The paper is organized as follows. In
Section

2
we review the parameter-dependent center manifold reduction technique in the context of ODEs and DDEs, including a brief overview of the sun-star calculus needed for DDEs. The existing solution cases for the linear operator equations used in
[
4
]
are insufficient when computing higher-order coefficients. Therefore, we have derived new, more general solutions which are presented in
Section

2.2.2
. In
Section

3
, we derive higher-order approximations of the
LPC
curve for the normal form, concluding with a discussion on the coefficients required in the parameter and center manifold approximations. The method previously used for deriving parameter-dependent coefficients in
[
16
]
and
[
4
]
does not extend to higher orders. Therefore, we used a slightly different approach, resulting in new equations for the parameter-dependent coefficients. These new equations are derived in
Section

4
for ODEs. In
Section

5
we explain how these equations will change in the DDE setting. Despite many similarities between the equations of both classes of differential equations, one must still be careful. For instance, one term vanishes in the ODE case, but persists for DDEs. Furthermore, we require an expression for the seventh-order critical normal form coefficient for the higher-order approximation. While this coefficient was previously derived in
[
18
]
, we rederived it here and identified a missing term in one of their published expressions, though it was included in their example calculations. Additionally, we extend this derivation to obtain the seventh-order coefficients for DDEs. All components of the new predictors are summarised in
Section

6
. The new equations have been implemented and their performance is shown through some examples in
Section

7
.

All of the computations were performed in Julia to create the examples. The corresponding code can be obtained upon request. The higher-order predictor for ODEs has also been implemented in the Julia software package BifurcationKit.

2
Parameter-dependent center manifold reduction and normalization

In this section, we review the general technique for computing the normal form coefficients on the parameter-dependent center manifold for ODEs and DDEs. For DDEs, this includes a summary of relevant results from sun-star calculus. The general methods used to solve the resulting linear systems are also discussed. This includes additional special cases of representations required to obtain the higher-order coefficients in DDEs.

2.1
The center manifold reduction and normalization method for ODEs

Consider a system of ODEs depending on two parameters

\dot{x}=F(x,\alpha),

(3)

where
x:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
F:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Throughout this paper, we assume that
F
is
C^{k}
-smooth for some sufficiently large integer
k
. Suppose that
x_{0}=0
is an equilibrium at
\alpha_{0}=0
, i.e.
F(0,0)=0
. We denote the Jacobian matrix at the equilibrium as
A=D_{x}F(0,0)
. Let
n_{c}
be the number of eigenvalues with zero real part and
T^{c}
the corresponding critical eigenspace. For the generalized Hopf bifurcation, we have
n_{c}=2
. A procedure to switch to the codim 1 bifurcation curves emanating from a codim 2 bifurcation point was introduced in
[
3
]
. We will follow the same approach below.

For each sufficiently small
\|\alpha\|
, system
Eq.

3
has a smooth local
n_{c}
-dimensional invariant center manifold
W^{c}_{\alpha}
. At
x=0
,
W_{0}^{c}
is tangent to the critical eigenspace
T^{c}
of
A
. Restricted to the center manifold, we can transform the system into a certain normal form using only smooth coordinate and parameter transformations:

\dot{w}=G(w,\beta),\quad G:\mathbb{R}^{n_{c}}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{n_{c}}.

(4)

These normal forms are known for all five codim 2 bifurcations, and details can be found, for example, in
[
15
, Chapter 8]
. From the normal form, we can derive an approximation for the codim 1 curves emanating from the codim 2 point. To relate the local behaviour of the normal form on the center manifold to our original system, we need a relation between the original parameters
\alpha
and the unfolding parameters
\beta
:

\alpha=K(\beta),\quad K:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}.

(5)

Furthermore, we need a parameterisation of the center manifold depending on the new parameters
\beta
:

x=H(w,\beta),\quad H:\mathbb{R}^{n_{c}}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}.

(6)

By substituting equations
\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:G},\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:K_gen}
and
Eq.

6
into equation
Eq.

3
, we find the
homological equation
:

H_{w}(w,\beta)G(w,\beta)=f(H(w,\beta),K(\beta)).

(7)

This is essentially a consequence of the invariance of the center manifold. In the following, we will use multi-indices
\nu
and
\mu
to simplify the notation in the expansions of
G,H
and
K
. A multi-index
\mu
is defined as
\mu=(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n})
for
\mu_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and we have that
w^{\mu}=w_{1}^{\mu_{1}}\ldots w_{n}^{\mu_{2}}
,
\mu!=\mu_{1}!\mu_{2}!\ldots\mu_{n}!
and
|\mu|=\mu_{1}+\ldots+\mu_{n}
. The general form of the normal form expansion is known and is expanded as

G(w,\beta)=\sum_{|\nu|+|\mu|\geq 1}\frac{1}{\nu!\mu!}g_{\nu\mu}w^{\nu}\beta^{\mu}.

(8)

Meanwhile,
H
and
K
are unknown and admit the expansions

H(w,\beta)=\sum_{|\nu|+|\mu|\geq 1}\frac{1}{\nu!\mu!}H_{\nu\mu}w^{\nu}\beta^{\mu},\quad K(\beta)=\sum_{|\mu|\geq 1}\frac{1}{\mu!}K_{\mu}\beta^{\mu}.

(9)

All of these expansions will be truncated at some sufficiently high order. For the Taylor expansion of
F
, we define the multilinear forms

\displaystyle B(u,v)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial^{2}F(x_{0},\alpha_{0})}{\partial x_{i}\partial x_{j}}u_{i}v_{j},\quad

\displaystyle J_{1}u=\sum_{i=1}^{2}\frac{\partial F(x_{0},\alpha_{0})}{\partial\alpha_{i}}u_{i},

\displaystyle C(u,v,w)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}\frac{\partial^{3}F(x_{0},\alpha_{0})}{\partial x_{i}\partial x_{j}\partial x_{k}}u_{i}v_{j}w_{k},\quad

\displaystyle A_{1}(u,v)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}F(x_{0},\alpha_{0})}{\partial x_{i}\partial\alpha_{j}}u_{i}v_{j},

\displaystyle B_{1}(u,v,w)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{2}\frac{\partial^{3}F(x_{0},\alpha_{0})}{\partial x_{i}\partial x_{j}\partial\alpha_{k}}u_{i}v_{j}w_{k}.\quad

etc.

We use the letters
A,B,C,D,E,K,
and
L
to denote (in increasing order) the derivatives of
F
with respect to its first argument evaluated at the critical point. The subscript denotes the order of the derivative of
F
with respect to the parameters.

Substituting the expansions
\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:expansion_G},\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:expansions_HK}
along with the Taylor expansion of
F
, into the homological equation
Eq.

7
and collecting coefficients of the
w^{\nu}\beta^{\mu}
-terms results in a set of linear equations. These can be solved recursively for the unknown coefficients
g_{\nu\mu},H_{\nu\mu}
, and
K_{\mu}
by applying the Fredholm solvability condition.

2.2
The center manifold reduction and normalization method for DDEs

As presented in
[
14
]
, the normalization technique for local bifurcations in ODEs can be lifted to the infinite-dimensional setting of DDEs. This normalization method was extended to include parameters in
[
4
]
. We will closely follow the procedure from
[
4
]
, summarizing results from sun-star calculus needed to apply this technique for the generalized Hopf bifurcation.

We take the nonreflexive Banach space
X:=C([-h,0],\mathbb{R}^{n})
and define for each
t\geq 0
the history function
x_{t}:[-h,0]\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
at time
t
as

x_{t}(\theta):=x(t+\theta),\quad\textrm{ for all }\theta\in[-h,0].

Consider the classical parameter-dependent DDE

\dot{x}(t)=F(x_{t},\alpha),\quad t\geq 0,

(10)

where
F:X\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a
C^{k}
-smooth operator for sufficiently large integer
k
and
0<h<\infty
.
Assume that system
Eq.

10
satisfies
F(0,0)=0
and that the trivial equilibrium undergoes a generalized Hopf bifurcation at
\alpha=0
. The existence of a center manifold for DDEs has been rigorously established within the framework of sun-star calculus
[
11
]
. As for ODEs, the proof relies on a variations-of-constants formula describing the solutions. For DDEs, it is convenient to formulate this in the larger space
X^{\odot\star}
, the sun-star dual space of
X
. Here, we provide only the definitions and results from sun-star calculus needed for the normalisation method. For a full introduction to sun-star calculus and proofs, we refer to
[
11
]
.

There exists a unique matrix-valued function of normalized bounded variation
\zeta:[0,h]\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
such that the linear part of
Eq.

10
at
\alpha=0
can written as

D_{1}F(0,0)\varphi=\langle\zeta,\varphi\rangle:=\int_{0}^{h}d\zeta(\theta)\varphi(-\theta).

(11)

The above integral is of Riemann-Stieltjes type. With this notation, we can express the right-hand side of
Eq.

10
in terms of its linear and nonlinear parts:

F(\varphi,\alpha)=\langle\zeta,\varphi\rangle+D_{2}F(0,0)\alpha+G(\varphi,\alpha),

where the nonlinear part
G
is a smooth operator satisfying
G(0,0)=0
,
D_{1}G(0,0)=0
, and
D_{2}G(0,0)=0
. Let
T
be the unique
C_{0}
-semigroup on
X
corresponding to the linear part of
Eq.

10
at
0\in X
for the critical parameter value
\alpha=0
. The generator
A
of the semigroup
T
is given by

A\varphi=\dot{\varphi},\quad\textrm{ with }\quad D(A)=\{\varphi\in C^{1}|\dot{\varphi}(0)=\langle\zeta,\varphi\rangle\}.

(12)

When we go to the dual space
X^{\star}
of
X
, we lose strong continuity with the adjoint semigroup
T^{\star}
. Therefore, we consider the maximal subspace of strong continuity
X^{\odot}
. The space
X^{\odot}
has the representation

X^{\odot}=\mathbb{R}^{n}\times L^{1}([0,h],\mathbb{R}^{n}),

(13)

with the duality pairing between
\varphi^{\odot}=(c,g)\in X^{\odot}
and
\varphi\in X
given by

\langle\varphi^{\odot},\varphi\rangle=c^{T}\varphi(0)+\int_{0}^{h}g(\theta)\varphi(-\theta)d\theta.

(14)

On the Banach space
X^{\odot}
we have a
C_{0}
-semigroup
T^{\odot}
with generator
A^{\odot}
. The dual space
X^{\odot\star}
of
X^{\odot}
has the representation

X^{\odot\star}=\mathbb{R}^{n}\times L^{\infty}([0,h],\mathbb{R}^{n}).

(15)

On this space, we have the generator

A^{\odot\star}(\alpha,\varphi)=(\langle\zeta,\varphi\rangle,\dot{\varphi}),\quad\textrm{ with }\quad D(A^{\odot\star})=\{(\alpha,\varphi)|\varphi\in\textrm{Lip}(\alpha)\},

(16)

where Lip
(\alpha)
denotes the subset of
L^{\infty}([-h,0],\mathbb{C})
consisting of Lipschitz continuous functions which assume the value
\alpha
at
\theta=0
. The duality pairing between
\varphi^{\odot\star}=(a,\psi)\in X^{\odot\star}
and
\varphi^{\odot}=(c,g)\in X^{\odot}
is given by

\langle\varphi^{\odot\star},\varphi^{\odot}\rangle=c^{T}a+\int_{0}^{h}g(\theta)\psi(-\theta)d\theta.

(17)

We look again at the maximal subspace of strong continuity
X^{\odot\odot}
. There exists an injection
j:X\to X^{\odot\odot}
defined by

j\varphi=(\varphi(0),\varphi)\in X^{\odot\odot}\textrm{ for all }\varphi\in X.

(18)

We have that
X^{\odot\odot}=j(X)
. This property is also known as
sun-reflexivity
. We will often move back and forth between the space
X
and its sun-dual space
X^{\odot\star}
.

Assume that there are
n_{0}\geq 1
eigenvalues of the linearization of
Eq.

10
at
\alpha=0
on the imaginary axis, with a corresponding real
n_{0}
-dimensional center eigenspace
X_{0}
. Then
[
4
, Corollary 20]
will imply the existence of a parameter-dependent local center manifold
\mathcal{W}_{\textrm{loc}}^{c}(\alpha)
for
Eq.

10
. As in the ODE case, we want to include a relation
\alpha=K(\beta)
between the original parameters
\alpha
and some new unfolding parameters
\beta
. Let
u:I\to X
with
u(t):=x_{t}\in\mathcal{W}_{\textrm{loc}}^{c}(\alpha)
be as in
[
4
, Corollary 20]
. Then,
u
is differentiable on
I
and satisfies the equation

j\dot{u}(t)=A^{\odot\star}ju(t)+(D_{2}F(0,0)K(\beta))r^{\odot\star}+G(u(t),K(\beta))r^{\odot\star},\quad\textrm{ for all }t\in I.

(19)

Here
wr^{\odot\star}=(w,0)\in X^{\odot\star}
, for
w\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Now, choose a basis
\Phi
of
X_{0}
. With respect to
\Phi
and in terms of the new parameter
\beta
, we can consider the locally defined
C^{k}
-smooth parameterization
H:\mathbb{R}^{n_{c}}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to X
of the center manifold
\mathcal{W}_{\textrm{loc}}^{c}(\alpha)
. Let
z(t)
be the coordinate with respect to
\Phi
of the projection of
u(t)
onto the center subspace
X_{0}
. Then
z:I\to\mathbb{R}^{n_{0}}
satisfies a parameter-dependent ODE where the right-hand side is a
C^{k}
-smooth vector field that can be expanded as

\dot{z}=\sum_{|\nu|+|\mu|\geq 1}\frac{1}{\nu!\mu!}g_{\nu\mu}z^{\nu}\beta^{\mu}.

(20)

We may assume that
Eq.

20
is a smooth normal form in terms of the unfolding parameters
\beta
. Furthermore, we have that

\displaystyle u(t)=H(z(t),\beta),\quad t\in I.

(21)

If we substitute equation
Eq.

21
into the equation
Eq.

19
, we find the following
homological equation
:

A^{\odot\star}jH(z,\beta)+(J_{1}K(\beta))r^{\odot\star}+G(H(z,\beta),K(\beta))r^{\odot\star}=jD_{z}H(z,\beta)\dot{z},

(22)

where
\dot{z}
is given by
Eq.

20
and we defined
J_{1}:=D_{2}F(0,0)
. The mappings
H
and
K
allow for the expansions

H(z,\beta)=\sum_{|\nu|+|\mu|\geq 1}\frac{1}{\nu!\mu!}H_{\nu\mu}z^{\nu}\beta^{\mu},\quad K(\beta)=\sum_{|\mu|\geq 1}\frac{1}{\mu!}K_{\mu}\beta^{\mu}.

(23)

Meanwhile, the nonlinear part
G(\varphi,\alpha)
can be expanded as

G(\varphi,\alpha)=\sum_{r+s>1}\frac{1}{r!s!}D_{1}^{r}D_{2}^{s}F(0,0)(\varphi^{(r)},\alpha^{(s)}),

(24)

where
\varphi^{(r)}=(\varphi,\ldots,\varphi)\in X^{r}
,
\alpha^{(s)}=(\alpha,\ldots,\alpha)\in[\mathbb{R}^{2}]^{s}
and
D_{1}^{r}D_{2}^{s}F(0,0):X^{r}\times[\mathbb{R}^{2}]^{s}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the mixed Fréchet derivative of order
r+s
evaluated at
(0,0)\in X\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Just as in the ODE-case, we will indicate the multilinear forms in the expansion of
F
with
B,C,D,E,K,L
and for the parameter-dependent derivatives
A_{i},B_{i},C_{i},
etc. where the subscript
i
indicated the number of derivatives with respect to the parameter. Thus,

\displaystyle J_{1}=D_{2}F(0,0),\quad

\displaystyle B(u,u)=D_{1}^{2}F(0,0)(u,u),

\displaystyle A_{1}(u,\alpha)=D_{1}^{1}D_{2}^{1}F(0,0)(u,\alpha),\quad

\displaystyle C(u,u,u)=D_{1}^{3}F(0,0)(u,u,u),

\displaystyle B_{1}(u,u,\alpha)=D_{1}^{2}D_{2}^{1}F(0,0)(u,u,\alpha),\quad

etc.

For the case of discrete DDEs, explicit formulas for the computation of the multilinear forms are presented in
[
4
, Section 5]
.

We can now substitute the expansions
\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:z},\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:expansions_HK_DDE}
and
Eq.

24
into the homological equation
Eq.

22
and collect terms of equal powers
z^{\nu}\beta^{\mu}
. Then it is possible to solve recursively for the coefficients
g_{\nu\mu},H_{\nu\mu}
and
K_{\mu}
. Before we discuss how we can solve the resulting equations, we need some facts about the spectrum of the generator
A
.

2.2.1
The spectrum

To determine if bifurcations are present, we need to analyse the spectrum of the generator
A
of the semigroup corresponding to the linear part of
Eq.

10
. For this, we need the
characteristic matrix function
which is defined as

\Delta(z)=zI-\int_{0}^{h}e^{-z\theta}d\zeta(\theta),\quad\Delta:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}^{n\times n},

(25)

and contains all spectral information about
A
. The integral in the above expression is of Riemann-Stieltjes type and
\zeta
is the same from
Eq.

11
. The eigenvalues of
A
are given by the roots of the
characteristic equation

\det\Delta(\lambda)=0.

(26)

We only have to concern ourselves with simple eigenvalues, i.e. eigenvalues for which both the geometric and algebraic multiplicities equal one. If
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
is a simple eigenvalue of
A
, there exist an eigenfunction
\varphi
and an adjoint eigenfunction
\varphi^{\odot}
such that

A\varphi=\lambda\varphi,\quad A^{\star}\varphi^{\odot}=\lambda\varphi^{\odot}.

Let
q,p\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
such that

\Delta(\lambda)q=0,\quad p^{T}\Delta(\lambda)=0.

Then the corresponding eigenfunctions are given by

\varphi(\theta)=e^{\lambda\theta}q,\quad\theta\in[-h,0]

(27)

and

\varphi^{\odot}=\left(p,\theta\mapsto p\int_{\theta}^{h}e^{\lambda(\theta-\tau)}d\zeta(\tau)\right),\quad\theta\in[-h,0].

(28)

Furthermore, it is possible to normalize the eigenfunctions to

\langle\varphi^{\odot},\varphi\rangle=p\Delta^{\prime}(\lambda)q=1.

Here
\Delta^{\prime}(\lambda)
is the derivative of
z\mapsto\Delta(z)
evaluated at
z=\lambda

\Delta^{\prime}(\lambda)=I+\int_{0}^{h}\theta e^{-\lambda\theta}d\zeta(\theta).

(29)

Proofs of the above results on the spectrum of
A
can be found in
[
11
, Chapter IV]
. Note that for
k\geq 2
we have the higher-order derivatives

\Delta^{(k)}(\lambda)=(-1)^{k+1}\int_{0}^{h}\theta^{k}e^{-\lambda\theta}d\zeta(\theta).

(30)

In the following, we will denote the second, third, and fourth derivatives as
\Delta^{\prime\prime}(\lambda)
,
\Delta^{\prime\prime\prime}(\lambda)
, and
\Delta^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}(\lambda)
respectively.

In the special case of the discrete DDE
Eq.

2
, the characteristic matrix is given by

\Delta(z)=zI-\sum_{j=0}^{m}M_{j}e^{-z\tau_{j}},\quad z\in\mathbb{C},

where
M_{j}:=D_{1,j}f(0,0)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is the partial derivative of
f
with respect to its
j
th state argument evaluated at the origin
[
4
, Section 6]
.

Remark

When dealing with the spectrum of
A
, it is necessary to complexify all of the above spaces and the linear operators acting on them. As previously remarked by
[
14
, Remark 2.2]
this is not a trivial task. Fortunately, this has already been carried out in detail in
[
11
, Section III.7]
and therefore will not be discussed here.

2.2.2
Solving the linear operator equations

From the homological equation
Eq.

22
we will find equations of the form

(\lambda I-A^{\odot\star})(v_{0},v)=(w_{0},w),

(31)

for some known
(w_{0},w)\in X^{\odot\star}
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
and an unknown
(v_{0},v)\in D(A^{\odot\star})
. We will only have to consider two cases, either
\lambda
is a simple eigenvalue or
\lambda
is not an eigenvalue.

If
\lambda
is not an eigenvalue, then equation
Eq.

31
has a unique solution

(v_{0},v)=(\lambda I-A^{\odot\star})^{-1}(w_{0},w).

(32)

To compute the solutions, we need a representation for the solution
(v_{0},v)
. The following lemma gives the general result.

{lemma}

[
[
14
]
, Lemma 3.3] Suppose that
\lambda
is not an eigenvalue. Then the unique solution of
Eq.

31
is given by

v(\theta)=e^{\lambda\theta}v_{0}+\int_{\theta}^{0}e^{\lambda(\theta-\sigma)}w(\sigma)d\sigma\quad(\theta\in[-h,0]),

(33)

with

v_{0}=\Delta^{-1}(\lambda)\left\{w_{0}+\int_{0}^{h}d\zeta(\tau)\int_{0}^{\tau}e^{-\lambda\sigma}w(\sigma-\tau)d\sigma\right\}.

(34)

We will only encounter equations where the right-hand side of
Eq.

31
has a form that allows us to express the above representations in terms of derivatives of the characteristic matrix function
\Delta(z)
. The following Corollary presents some useful cases that follow from the application of
Section

2.2.2
.

{corollary}

Suppose that
\lambda
is not an eigenvalue. We have the following special cases:

1.

[
4
, Lemma 3]
Suppose that
(w_{0},w)=\left(w_{0},\theta\mapsto e^{\lambda\theta}\Delta^{-1}(\lambda)\eta\right)
for some fixed
\eta\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Then the unique solution
(v_{0},v)\in D(A^{\odot\star})
of
Eq.

31
has the representation

v_{0}=v(0),\quad v(\theta)=\Delta^{-1}(\lambda)\left(e^{\lambda\theta}w_{0}+[\Delta^{\prime}(\lambda)-I-\theta\Delta(\lambda)]w(\theta)\right).

2.

Let
k\geq 1
. Suppose that
(w_{0},w)=\left(0,\theta\mapsto\theta^{k}e^{\lambda\theta}\Delta^{-1}(\lambda)\eta\right)
for some fixed
\eta\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Then the unique solution
(v_{0},v)\in D(A^{\odot\star})
of
Eq.

31
has the representation

v_{0}=v(0),\quad v(\theta)=\frac{1}{k+1}e^{\lambda\theta}\Delta^{-1}(\lambda)[\Delta^{(k+1)}(\lambda)-\theta^{k+1}\Delta(\lambda)]\Delta^{-1}(\lambda)\eta.

The special cases from
Section

2.2.2
can be combined to arrive at the following result:

{corollary}

Suppose that
\lambda
is not an eigenvalue and that the right-hand side of
Eq.

31
has the representation

(w_{0},w)=\left(w_{0},\theta\mapsto e^{\lambda\theta}\Delta^{-1}(\lambda)[\eta+\theta\xi_{1}+\theta^{2}\xi_{2}]\right),

for some fixed
\eta,\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Then the unique solution
(v_{0},v)\in D(A^{\odot\star})
of
Eq.

31
has the following representation

\displaystyle v(\theta)

\displaystyle=e^{\lambda\theta}\Delta^{-1}(\lambda)\Bigl(w_{0}+[\Delta^{\prime}(\lambda)-I-\theta\Delta(\lambda)]\Delta^{-1}(\lambda)\eta

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}[\Delta^{\prime\prime}(\lambda)-\theta^{2}\Delta(\lambda)]\Delta^{-1}(\lambda)\xi_{1}+\frac{1}{3}[\Delta^{\prime\prime\prime}(\lambda)-\theta^{3}\Delta(\lambda)]\Delta^{-1}(\lambda)\xi_{2}\Bigr),

and
v_{0}=v(0)
.

Proof.

Write

\displaystyle(w_{0},w)

\displaystyle=\left(w_{0},\theta\mapsto e^{\lambda\theta}\Delta^{-1}(z)\eta\right)+\left(0,\theta\mapsto\theta e^{\lambda\theta}\Delta^{-1}(\lambda)\xi_{1}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(0,\theta\mapsto\theta^{2}e^{\lambda\theta}\Delta^{-1}(\lambda)\xi_{2}\right).

Using the linearity of the inverse operator
(\lambda I-A^{\odot\star})^{-1}
we can apply the cases from
Section

2.2.2
.
∎

Finally, from this result, we can derive the following special case, which will aid our calculations in
Section

5
.

{corollary}

Suppose that
\lambda
is not an eigenvalue. We have the following special case:
Suppose that
(w_{0},w)
has the form

\displaystyle w_{0}=w(0),\quad w(\theta)=e^{\lambda\theta}\Delta^{-1}(\lambda)\left(M+[\Delta^{\prime}(\lambda)-\theta\Delta(\lambda)]\hat{\eta}+[\Delta^{\prime\prime}(\lambda)-\theta^{2}\Delta(\lambda)]\hat{\xi}\right),

for some fixed
M,\hat{\eta},\hat{\xi}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Then the unique solution
(v_{0},v)\in D(A^{\odot\star})
of
Eq.

31
has the representation

\displaystyle v(\theta)=e^{\lambda\theta}\Delta^{-1}(\lambda)\left([\Delta^{\prime}(\lambda)-\theta\Delta(\lambda)]w(0)-\frac{1}{2}[\Delta^{\prime\prime}(\lambda)-\theta^{2}\Delta(\lambda)]\hat{\eta}-\frac{1}{3}[\Delta^{\prime\prime\prime}(\lambda)-\theta^{3}\Delta(\lambda)]\hat{\xi}\right).

and
v_{0}=v(0)
.

Proof.

We can rewrite
w(\theta)
as

w(\theta)=e^{\lambda\theta}\Delta^{-1}(\lambda)[M+\Delta^{\prime}(\lambda)\hat{\eta}+\Delta^{\prime\prime}(\lambda)\hat{\xi}-\theta\Delta(\lambda)\hat{\eta}-\theta^{2}\Delta(\lambda)\hat{\xi}].

Now we can apply
Section

2.2.2
by taking
\eta=M+\Delta^{\prime}(\lambda)\hat{\eta}+\Delta^{\prime\prime}(\lambda)\hat{\xi}
,
\xi_{1}=-\Delta(\lambda)\hat{\eta}
and
\xi_{2}=-\Delta(\lambda)\hat{\xi}
. Finally, use that
w_{0}=\Delta^{-1}(\lambda)\eta
to simplify the expression.
∎

If
\lambda
is an eigenvalue, system
Eq.

31
does not have a unique solution. As in the ODE case, a variant of the Fredholm solvability condition exists for operator equations of the form
Eq.

31
:

{lemma}

[
[
14
]
, Lemma 3.2] For arbitrary
\lambda
, a solution
(v_{0},v)\in D(A^{\odot\star})
to system
Eq.

31
exists if and only if

\langle(w_{0},w),\varphi^{\odot}\rangle=0,\quad\textrm{ for all }\quad\varphi^{\odot}\in N(\lambda I-A^{\star}).

During our computations in
Section

5
we will refer to it as the Fredholm solvability condition. Similarly to before, we can use a bordered
operator
inverse

(\lambda I-A^{\odot\star})^{INV}:R(\lambda I-A^{\odot\star})\to D(A^{\odot\star})

to find a unique solution to system
Eq.

31
satisfying
\langle(v_{0},v),\varphi^{\odot}\rangle=0
for all
(w_{0},w)
for which
Eq.

31
is consistent, i.e. satisfies the Fredholm solvability condition. A general representation for
(\lambda I-A^{\odot\star})^{INV}
in the case that
\lambda
is a simple eigenvalue is given by the following proposition

Proposition 1

(
[
14
]
, Proposition 3.6)
.

Let
\lambda
be a simple eigenvalue of
A
with eigenvector
\varphi
and adjoint eigenvector
\varphi^{\odot}
, such that
\langle\varphi^{\odot},\varphi\rangle=1
. Suppose that
Eq.

31
is consistent for some given
(w_{0},w)\in X^{\odot\star}
. Then the unique solution
(v_{0},v)=(\lambda I-A^{\odot\star})^{INV}(w_{0},w)
satisfying
\langle(v_{0},v),\varphi^{\odot}\rangle=0
is given by

v_{0}=\xi+\gamma q,\quad v(\theta)=e^{\lambda\theta}v_{0}+\int_{\theta}^{0}e^{\lambda(\theta-\sigma)}w(\sigma)d\sigma\quad(\theta\in[-h,0]),

(35)

with

\xi=\Delta(\lambda)^{INV}\left[w_{0}+\int_{0}^{h}d\zeta(\tau)\int_{0}^{\tau}e^{-\lambda\sigma}w(\sigma-\tau)d\sigma\right],

(36)

and the constant
\gamma
is given by

\gamma=-p\Delta^{\prime}(\lambda)\xi-p\int_{0}^{h}\int_{\tau}^{h}e^{-\lambda s}d\zeta(s)\int_{-\tau}^{0}e^{-\lambda\sigma}w(\sigma)d\sigma d\tau.

(37)

The expression for
\xi
contains the bordered matrix inverse
\Delta(\lambda)^{INV}
. A unique solution
x=\Delta(\lambda)^{INV}y
satisfying
p^{T}x=0
can be found by solving the following bordered system

\begin{pmatrix}\Delta(\lambda)&q\\
p^{T}&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}x\\
s\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}y\\
0\end{pmatrix},

for
(x,s)\in\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
. The derivation of the higher order coefficients requires additional cases for the representation of
(\lambda I-A^{\odot\star})^{INV}
than the one presented in
[
4
]
. A more general case follows from applying
1
.

{corollary}

Suppose in addition to the assumptions from
1
that
(w_{0},w)
satisfies

(w_{0},w)=\left(w_{0},e^{\lambda\theta}(\xi_{0}+\theta\xi_{1}+\ldots+\theta^{m}\xi_{m})\right),

for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
and constant vectors
\xi_{k}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
0\leq k\leq m
.
Then the unique solution
(v_{0},v)=(\lambda I-A^{\odot\star})^{INV}(w_{0},w)
satisfying
\langle(v_{0},v),\varphi^{\odot}\rangle=0
is given by

v_{0}=\xi+\gamma q,\quad v(\theta)=e^{\lambda\theta}\left(v_{0}-\theta\xi_{0}-\frac{1}{2}\theta^{2}\xi_{1}-\ldots-\frac{1}{m+1}\theta^{m+1}\xi_{m}\right),

(38)

where

\xi=\Delta(\lambda)^{INV}\left[w_{0}+[\Delta^{\prime}(\lambda)-I]\xi_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\Delta^{\prime\prime}(\lambda)\xi_{1}+\ldots+\frac{1}{m+1}\Delta^{(m+1)}(\lambda)\xi_{m}\right],

and

\gamma=-p\Delta^{\prime}(\lambda)\xi+\frac{1}{2}p\Delta^{\prime\prime}(\lambda)\xi_{0}+\frac{1}{6}p\Delta^{\prime\prime\prime}(\lambda)\xi_{1}+\ldots+\frac{1}{(m+1)(m+2)}\Delta^{(m+2)}(\lambda)\xi_{m}.

Remark

In
Section

5
, we will encounter
(w_{0},w)
of the form

w_{0}=\eta+\xi_{0},\quad w(\theta)=e^{\lambda\theta}(\xi_{0}+\theta\xi_{1}+\ldots+\theta^{m}\xi_{m}).

For the bordered inverse of this case, we will use the notation
v=B_{\lambda}^{INV}(\eta,\xi_{0},\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{m})
. For instance, if
(w_{0},w)=(\eta,0)+\kappa(q,\varphi)
, we write
v=B_{\lambda}^{INV}(\eta,\kappa q)
.

3
Higher order
LPC
curve approximation for the normal form

In this section, we first derive a parameter approximation of the
LPC
curve for the normal form, followed by an approximation of the cycle period along this curve. We conclude with a detailed discussion of the necessary coefficients for the center manifold and parameter approximations when generalising to
n
-dimensional systems of ODEs.

3.1
Approximmation of the
LPC
curve derived from the normal form

Assume that at
\alpha=0
there is an equilibrium at the origin with only one pair of purely imaginary simple eigenvalues

\lambda_{1,2}=\pm i\omega_{0},\quad\omega_{0}>0.

Restricted to the center manifold near a generalized Hopf bifurcation, the system
Eq.

3
can be transformed to the following parameter-dependent normal form

\dot{w}=\lambda(\alpha)w+c_{1}(\alpha)w|w|^{2}+c_{2}(\alpha)w|w|^{4}+c_{3}(\alpha)w|w|^{6}+O(|w|^{8}),\quad w\in\mathbb{C},

where
\lambda(0)=i\omega_{0}
,
d_{1}=\Re{(c_{1}(0))}=0
and
d_{2}=\Re{(c_{2}(0))}\neq 0
. Furthermore, we have the following
transversaility
condition:

\textrm{The map }\alpha\mapsto(\Re{(\lambda(\alpha))},\Re{(c_{1}(\alpha))})\textrm{ is regular at }\alpha=0.

(39)

If this condition is satisfied, we may introduce new parameters:

(\beta_{1}(\alpha),\beta_{2}(\alpha))=(\Re{(\lambda(\alpha))},\Re{(c_{1}(\alpha))}).

Then, for
\|\alpha\|
small enough, the normal form can be expressed in terms of
\beta
in the following way

\dot{w}=(i\omega_{0}+\beta_{1}+ib_{1}(\beta))w+(\beta_{2}+ib_{2}(\beta))w|w|^{2}+c_{2}(\beta)w|w|^{4}+c_{3}(\beta)w|w|^{6}+O(|w|^{8}),

(40)

where
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
are real valued functions with
b_{1}(0)=0
and
b_{2}(0)=\Im{(c_{1}(0))}
. Note that we write
c_{i}(\beta)
instead of
c_{i}(\alpha(\beta))
for convenience. To get a higher-order approximation of the
LPC
curve, we first substitute
w=\rho e^{i\psi}
into
Eq.

40
. Taking the real part yields the following amplitude equation

\displaystyle\dot{\rho}

\displaystyle=\rho(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}\rho^{2}+\Re{}(c_{2}(\beta))\rho^{4}+\Re{}(c_{3}(\beta))\rho^{6}+O(\rho^{7})).

(41)

3.1.1
LPC curve in the amplitude equation

To derive an approximation for the
LPC
curve we expand

\displaystyle\Re{}(c_{2}(\beta))

\displaystyle=d_{2}+a_{3210}\beta_{1}+a_{3201}\beta_{2}+O(\|\beta\|^{2}),

\displaystyle\Re{}(c_{3}(\beta))

\displaystyle=d_{3}+O(\|\beta\|).

The
LPC
curve corresponds to a double equilibrium of the right-hand side of equation
Eq.

41
, which occurs when both the right-hand side and its first derivative with respect to
\rho
vanish. This leads to the following system of equations:

\begin{cases}\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}\rho^{2}+\Re{}(c_{2}(\beta))\rho^{4}+\Re{}(c_{3}(\beta))\rho^{6}+O(\rho^{7})=0,\\
\beta_{2}\rho+2\Re{}(c_{2}(\beta))\rho^{3}+3\Re{}(c_{3}(\beta))\rho^{5}+O(\rho^{6})=0.\end{cases}

(42)

Let us define
P:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
by

P(\rho,\beta)=\begin{pmatrix}\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}\rho^{2}+\Re{}(c_{2}(\beta))\rho^{4}+\Re{}(c_{3}(\beta))\rho^{6}+O(\rho^{7})\\
\beta_{2}+2\Re{}(c_{2}(\beta))\rho^{2}+3\Re{}(c_{3}(\beta))\rho^{4}+O(\rho^{5})\end{pmatrix}.

(43)

Equation
Eq.

42
is equivalent to
P(\rho,\beta)=0
. Note that
P(0,0)=0
and
DP_{\beta}(0,0)
is invertible. Thus, by the Implicit Function Theorem, there exists a unique, locally smooth function
\beta(\rho)=(\beta_{1}(\rho),\beta_{2}(\rho))
near
\rho=0
such that
\beta(0)=0
and
P(\rho,\beta(\rho))=0
. Consequently, we can expand
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
as power series in
\rho
:

\displaystyle\beta_{i}

\displaystyle=m_{i,1}\rho+m_{i,2}\rho^{2}+m_{i,3}\rho^{3}+m_{i,4}\rho^{4}+m_{i,5}\rho^{5}+m_{i,6}\rho^{6}+O(\rho^{7}),\quad i=1,2.

We can solve for the unknown coefficients by substituting these expansions into
P(\rho,\beta)=0
and collecting terms up to order
\rho^{6}
in the first equation and
\rho^{4}
in the second. This yields the following asymptotic expansions for the
\beta
parameters:

\displaystyle\beta_{1}

\displaystyle=d_{2}\rho^{4}+2(d_{3}-a_{3201}d_{2})\rho^{6}+O(\rho^{7}),

\displaystyle\beta_{2}

\displaystyle=-2d_{2}\rho^{2}+(4a_{3201}d_{2}-3d_{3})\rho^{4}+O(\rho^{5}).

For small
\rho=\varepsilon>0
an approximation of the
LPC
curve is given by:

(\rho,\beta_{1},\beta_{2})=(\varepsilon,d_{2}\varepsilon^{4}+2(d_{3}-a_{3201}d_{2})\varepsilon^{6}+O(\varepsilon^{7}),-2d_{2}\varepsilon^{2}+(4a_{3201}d_{2}-3d_{3})\varepsilon^{4}+O(\varepsilon^{5})).

(44)

3.1.2
Period approximmation

To approximate the period
T
of the cycles on the
LPC
curve near the generalized Hopf bifurcation we use that

\int_{0}^{T}\dot{\psi}dt=2\pi.

(45)

To obtain the approximation of
\dot{\psi}
, we first take the imaginary part of equation
Eq.

40
after substituting
w=\rho e^{i\psi}
and partially truncate higher order terms. This results in the equation:

\displaystyle\dot{\psi}

\displaystyle=\omega_{0}+b_{1}(\beta)+b_{2}(\beta)\rho^{2}+\Im{(c_{2}(0))}\rho^{4}+\Im{(c_{3}(0))}\rho^{6}.

(46)

For
b_{1}(\beta)
and
b_{2}(\beta)
we make the following expansions

\displaystyle b_{1}(\beta)

\displaystyle=b_{1,10}\beta_{1}+b_{1,01}\beta_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}+\frac{1}{2}b_{1,20}\beta_{1}^{2}+b_{1,11}\beta_{1}\beta_{2}}+\frac{1}{2}b_{1,02}\beta_{2}^{2}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}+O(\|\beta\|^{3})},

\displaystyle b_{2}(\beta)

\displaystyle=\Im{(c_{1}(0))}+{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}b_{2,10}\beta_{1}}+b_{2,01}\beta_{2}+{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}O(\|\beta\|^{2})}.

Since we are only interested in an approximation of the period up to fourth order in
\varepsilon
, the terms in grey would not appear in the period approximation. Their inclusion here is meant to demonstrate this explicitly. Now, substitute the parameter expansions
Eq.

44
of the
LPC
curve into equation
Eq.

46
and solve the integral
Eq.

45
. For small
\rho=\varepsilon>0
this yields the following period approximation:

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle=2\pi/(\omega_{0}+(\Im{(c_{1}(0))}-2d_{2}b_{1,01})\varepsilon^{2}+[d_{2}b_{1,10}+(4a_{3201}d_{2}-3d_{3})b_{1,01}+2d_{2}^{2}b_{1,02}

\displaystyle-2d_{2}b_{2,01}+\Im{(c_{2}(0))}]\varepsilon^{4}+O(\varepsilon^{5})).

(47)

3.2
Coefficients in the center manifold and parameter transformation approximmations

To find all the coefficients that need to be included in the approximations
K
and
H
, we must determine which terms in the normal form
Eq.

40
affect the
LPC
curve approximation
Eq.

44
up to the desired order. As noted in
[
5
]
, it is equally important to account for the influence of terms
not
included in the normal form. Any term affecting the
LPC
curve approximation
Eq.

44
must be eliminated, i.e. transformed into higher-order terms, to maintain the accuracy of our predictor at the intended order.

For example, consider a term like
w\beta_{2}^{2}
appearing in the normal form after reducing the system to the center manifold. If we derive the parameter approximation for the
LPC
curve to the same order as
Eq.

44
, following the method outlined in
Section

3.1.1
, this will introduce two additional terms in the expression for
\beta_{1}
. Thus,
w\beta_{2}^{2}
impacts the
LPC
curve approximation, indicating that it should be transformed away. To achieve this, we need to include the coefficient
K_{02}
in the parameter approximation
Eq.

5
.

To derive our parameter approximation of the
LPC
curve it is enough to consider the truncated normal form:

\displaystyle\dot{w}

\displaystyle=(i\omega_{0}+\beta_{1}+ib_{1}(\beta))w+(\beta_{2}+ib_{2}(\beta))w|w|^{2}+(c_{2}(0)+a_{3201}\beta_{2})w|w|^{4}+d_{3}w|w|^{6}.

We now include terms of the form
g_{nmkl}w^{n}\bar{w}^{m}\beta_{1}^{k}\beta_{2}^{l}
for
n,m,k,l\in\mathbb{N}
with
n+m+k+l\geq 1
to this normal form, and determine if they influence the parameter approximation.

The amplitude equation
\dot{\rho}
is derived by taking the real part of the normal form after substituting
w=\rho e^{i\psi}
. All terms for which
n-m-1\neq 0
will be resonant. These will certainly influence the predictor up to a certain order. Let us write
a_{nmkl}(\psi)=\Re{}\{g_{nmkl}e^{i\psi(n-m-1)}\}
. Then the amplitude equation will have the following form

\displaystyle\dot{\rho}=\beta_{1}\rho+\beta_{2}\rho^{3}+(d_{2}+a_{3201}\beta_{2})\rho^{5}+d_{3}\rho^{7}+a_{nmkl}(\psi)\rho^{n+m}\beta_{1}^{k}\beta_{2}^{l}.

To derive a parameter approximation of the
LPC
curve, we look for a double zero in the equation

\displaystyle\beta_{1}\rho+\beta_{2}\rho^{3}+(d_{2}+a_{3201}\beta_{2})\rho^{5}+d_{3}\rho^{7}+a_{nmkl}(\psi)\rho^{n+m}\beta_{1}^{k}\beta_{2}^{l}=0.

(48)

The goal is to determine the combinations of
n,m,k,l
for which the coefficient
a_{nmkl}(\psi)
will appear in our expansions of
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
, consequently affecting our
LPC
curve approximation. This will indicate which terms need to be removed in the normal form and thus which coefficients
H_{nmkl}
,
K_{kl}
need to be included in the center manifold and parameter approximations
[
5
]
. We will first discuss the case when
n+m=0
, and then provide the coefficients for
n+m\geq 1
.

Case
n+m=0:
The equations that need to be satisfied for a double zero are in this case given by

\displaystyle\beta_{1}\rho+\beta_{2}\rho^{3}+(d_{2}+a_{3201}\beta_{2})\rho^{5}+d_{3}\rho^{7}+a_{00kl}(\psi)\beta_{1}^{k}\beta_{2}^{l}

\displaystyle=0,

(49)

\displaystyle\beta_{1}+3\beta_{2}\rho^{2}+5(d_{2}+a_{3201}\beta_{2})\rho^{4}+7d_{3}\rho^{6}=0.

(50)

As before, we expand

\displaystyle\beta_{i}

\displaystyle=m_{i,1}\rho+m_{i,2}\rho^{2}+m_{i,3}\rho^{3}+m_{i,4}\rho^{4}+m_{i,5}\rho^{5}+m_{i,6}\rho^{6}+O(\rho^{7}),\quad i=1,2.

From equation
Eq.

50
it follows that
m_{1,1}=m_{1,2}=0
. We can simplify things a bit by setting
m_{i,1}=m_{i,3}=m_{i,5}=0
for
i=1,2
. To see why, let us examine all the
\rho
-terms in equation
Eq.

49
. This yields

a_{0001}(\psi)m_{2,1}+a_{0010}(\psi)m_{1,1}=0.

Since this must hold for all
\psi
, it follows that
m_{2,1}
and
m_{1,1}
have to equal zero. These terms are already zero in our
LPC
approximation, so this does not present a problem. Now collect all
\rho^{2}
-terms in the first equation. Using that
m_{2,1}=m_{1,1}=0
, we find the following equation

a_{0001}(\psi)m_{2,2}+a_{0010}(\psi)m_{1,2}=0.

This can only hold for all
\psi
if
m_{1,2}=m_{2,2}=0
. For
m_{1,2},
this is not an issue since this term is already zero in the approximation. However, for
m_{2,2}
, it is a problem because this term is not zero in our
LPC
curve approximation. Thus, if a term
g_{0001}\beta_{2}
is present in the normal form, it will influence our predictor. Therefore, this term must be removed, implying that the coefficients
H_{0001}
and
K_{01}
must be included in the center manifold and parameter approximations. Whenever a coefficient like
m_{1,3}
, which is zero in our
LPC
approximation, appears in front of a term
a_{00kl}(\psi)
, this term can be removed by simply setting
m_{1,3}=0
without altering the predictor. There is only a problem if the coefficient in front of
a_{00kl}(\psi)
depends on coefficients that are nonzero in our predictor. Thus, it is enough to substitute

\beta_{1}=m_{1,4}\rho^{4}+m_{1,6}\rho^{6},\textrm{ and }\beta_{2}=m_{2,2}\rho^{2}+m_{2,4}\rho^{4},

(51)

when collecting terms.
To arrive at the desired order of the predictor, we need to collect terms up to
\rho^{7}
in the first equation and terms up to
\rho^{6}
in the second equation. Therefore, all terms with
k,j\in\mathbb{N}
for which
\beta_{1}^{k}\beta_{2}^{l}
contains a term
\rho^{j}
,
j\leq 7
need to be removed. This implies that we need the coefficients
H_{0010},H_{0001},H_{0002},H_{0011}
, and
H_{0003}
in the center manifold approximation and the coefficients
K_{10},K_{01},K_{02},K_{11}
, and
K_{03}
in the parameter approximation.

Case
n+m\geq 1:
Now consider the following two equations

\displaystyle\beta_{1}\rho+\beta_{2}\rho^{3}+(d_{2}+a_{3201}\beta_{2})\rho^{5}+d_{3}\rho^{7}+a_{nmkl}(\psi)\rho^{n+m}\beta_{1}^{k}\beta_{2}^{l}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\beta_{1}+3\beta_{2}\rho^{2}+5(d_{2}+a_{3201}\beta_{2})\rho^{4}+7d_{3}\rho^{6}+a_{nmkl}(\psi)(n+m-1)\rho^{n+m-1}\beta_{1}^{k}\beta_{2}^{l}

\displaystyle=0.

By the same reasoning as in the previous case it is enough to substitute
Eq.

51
. To derive the parameter approximation of the predictor up to sixth order in
\rho
, we need to collect all terms up to
\rho^{7}
in the first equation and all terms up to
\rho^{6}
in the second equation. If the term
a_{nmkl}(\psi)\rho^{n+m}\beta_{1}^{k}\beta_{2}^{l}
contains terms of order
\rho^{7}
or lower, this will influence the approximation.

If
k=l=0
, then a term
a_{nmkl}(\psi)
will influence the predictor for all
1\leq n+m\leq 7
and thus all of these need to be removed. This implies that we need to include all coefficients
H_{nm00}
with
1\leq n+m\leq 7
in the center manifold approximation.

Now we consider the cases for which
k
or
l
are nonzero. For
n+m=1
, we need to remove all
a_{nmkl}(\psi)\rho\beta_{1}^{k}\beta_{2}^{l}
terms for which
\beta_{1}^{k}\beta_{2}^{l}
contains terms of
\rho^{6}
or less. This is the case for
(kl)=(10),(01),(02),(11)
and
(03)
. So we need
H_{nm10},H_{nm01},H_{nm02},H_{nm11}
and
H_{nm03}
with
n+m=1
. Continuing in this way, we find that all coefficients that need to be included in the center manifold approximation are

\displaystyle H_{nm10},H_{nm01},H_{nm02},H_{nm11},H_{nm03},

\displaystyle n+m

\displaystyle=j,j\in\{0,1\},

\displaystyle H_{nm10},H_{nm01},H_{nm02},

\displaystyle n+m

\displaystyle=j,j\in\{2,3\},

\displaystyle H_{nm01},

\displaystyle n+m

\displaystyle=j,j\in\{4,5\},

\displaystyle H_{nm00},

\displaystyle n+m

\displaystyle=j,j\in\{1,2,3,4,5,6,7\}.

Finally, all coefficients that need to be included in the parameter transformation are
K_{10}
,
K_{01}
,
K_{02}
,
K_{11}
, and
K_{03}
.

4
Coefficients of the parameter-dependent normal form and the predictor for ODEs

In this section, we derive equations for the coefficients that we need in our predictor for ODEs. Assume that system
Eq.

3
has an equilibrium at the origin at
\alpha=(0,0)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
with only one pair of purely imaginary simple eigenvalues

\lambda_{1,2}=\pm i\omega_{0},\quad\omega_{0}>0.

Additionally, we assume that all the other eigenvalues have non-zero real parts. This allows us to introduce the complex eigenvectors
p,q\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
satisfying

Aq=i\omega_{0}q,\quad A^{T}p=-i\omega_{0}p,\textrm{ and }\bar{q}^{T}q=\bar{p}^{T}q=1.

Furthermore, we assume that the first Lyapunov coefficient
l_{1}(0)=0
and the second Lyapunov coefficient
l_{2}(0)\neq 0
. The critical real eigenspace
T^{c}
corresponding to
\lambda_{1,2}
is now two dimensional and we can represent each
y\in T^{c}
in terms of the complex coordinate
w=\langle p,y\rangle
as

y=wq+\bar{w}\bar{q}.

Then, the homological equation
Eq.

7
has the form

D_{w}H(w,\bar{w},\beta)\dot{w}+D_{\bar{w}}H(w,\bar{w},\beta)\dot{\bar{w}}=F(H(w,\bar{w},\beta),K(\beta)).

(52)

Under the assumption that the transversality condition
Eq.

39
is satisfied, the truncated normal form restricted to the two-dimensional center manifold can be expressed in terms of the unfolding parameters
\beta=(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
:

\displaystyle\dot{w}

\displaystyle=(i\omega_{0}+\beta_{1}+ib_{1}(\beta))w+(\beta_{2}+ib_{2}(\beta))w|w|^{2}+(c_{2}(0)+g_{3201}\beta_{2})w|w|^{4}

\displaystyle+c_{3}(0)w|w|^{6},

(53)

where it is enough to expand

\displaystyle b_{1}(\beta)

\displaystyle=b_{1,10}\beta_{1}+b_{1,01}\beta_{2}+b_{1,11}\beta_{1}\beta_{2}+\frac{1}{2}b_{1,02}\beta_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{6}b_{1,03}\beta_{2}^{3},

(54)

\displaystyle b_{2}(\beta)

\displaystyle=\Im{(c_{1}(0))}+b_{2,10}\beta_{1}+b_{2,01}\beta_{2}+b_{2,11}\beta_{1}\beta_{2}+\frac{1}{2}b_{2,02}\beta_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{6}b_{2,03}\beta_{2}^{3}.

(55)

Under the assumption that
F
is sufficiently smooth, the truncated Taylor expansion of
F
is taken as

\displaystyle F(x,\alpha)

\displaystyle=Ax+J_{1}\alpha+A_{1}(x,\alpha)+\frac{1}{2}B(x,x)+\frac{1}{2}J_{2}(\alpha,\alpha)+\frac{1}{6}C(x,x,x)+\frac{1}{2}B_{1}(x,x,\alpha)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}A_{2}(x,\alpha,\alpha)+\frac{1}{6}J_{3}(\alpha,\alpha,\alpha)+\frac{1}{24}D(x,x,x,x)+\frac{1}{6}C_{1}(x,x,x,\alpha)+\frac{1}{4}B_{2}(x,x,\alpha,\alpha)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{6}A_{3}(x,\alpha,\alpha,\alpha)+\frac{1}{120}E(x,x,x,x,x)+\frac{1}{24}D_{1}(x,x,x,x,\alpha)+\frac{1}{12}C_{2}(x,x,x,\alpha,\alpha)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{12}B_{3}(x,x,\alpha,\alpha,\alpha)+\frac{1}{720}K(x,x,x,x,x,x)+\frac{1}{120}E_{1}(x,x,x,x,x,\alpha)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{36}C_{3}(x,x,x,\alpha,\alpha,\alpha)+\frac{1}{5040}L(x,x,x,x,x,x,x).

(56)

The parameterization of the center manifold is expanded as

\displaystyle H(w,\bar{w},\beta)

\displaystyle=qw+\bar{q}\bar{w}+\sum_{n+m=2}^{7}\frac{1}{n!m!}H_{nm00}w^{n}\bar{w}^{m}+\sum_{n+m=0}^{5}H_{nm01}\frac{1}{n!m!}w^{n}\bar{w}^{m}\beta_{2}

\displaystyle+\sum_{n+m=0}^{3}\frac{1}{n!m!}H_{nm10}w^{n}\bar{w}^{m}\beta_{1}+\sum_{n+m=0}^{3}\frac{1}{2n!m!}H_{nm02}w^{n}\bar{w}^{m}\beta_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle+\sum_{n+m=0}^{1}\frac{1}{n!m!}H_{nm11}w^{n}\bar{w}^{m}\beta_{1}\beta_{2}+\sum_{n+m=0}^{1}\frac{1}{6n!m!}H_{nm03}w^{n}\bar{w}^{m}\beta_{2}^{3}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}+\frac{1}{6}H_{1103}w\bar{w}\beta_{2}^{3}+\frac{1}{12}H_{2003}w^{2}\beta_{2}^{3}+\frac{1}{12}H_{2103}w^{2}\bar{w}\beta_{2}^{3}}.

(57)

Note that the image of
H
lies in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and thus we have that
H_{jk\mu}=\overline{H}_{kj\mu}
. The last three terms, marked in grey, are not needed to approximate the periodic orbit in phase space. However, as will be clear in
Section

4.2
, we do need the expressions for
H_{1103},H_{2003}
and
H_{2103}
to derive the coefficients
K_{03},H_{0003}
and
H_{1003}
. The relation between the parameters is expanded as

\displaystyle K(\beta)

\displaystyle=K_{10}\beta_{1}+K_{01}\beta_{2}+\frac{1}{2}K_{02}\beta_{2}^{2}+K_{11}\beta_{1}\beta_{2}+\frac{1}{6}K_{03}\beta_{2}^{3}.

(58)

Although the coefficients
b_{1,11}
,
b_{1,03}
in the expansion of
b_{1}(\beta)
and
b_{2,02},b_{2,11},b_{2,03}
in de expansion of
b_{2}(\beta)
do not appear in our predictor, we will need their expressions from the homological equation to solve for
K_{02},K_{11}
and
K_{03}
. All the equations collected from the homological equation
Eq.

52
are listed in the supplementary material (S2).

An overview of all the coefficients that must be determined for the higher-order predictor is given in
Fig.

2
. In the next two subsections, we derive all necessary critical normal form coefficients from
Section

4
and parameter-dependent coefficients from
Eq.

58
. The remaining center manifold coefficients are presented in the supplementary material (S1.3).

Figure 2:
Schematic overview of all the coefficients that need to be determined.

4.1
Critical normal form coefficients

The critical normal form coefficients up to fifth order have been derived in previous works. For completeness, we include the derivation following
[
15
, Section 8.7.3]
. The expression for the first Lyapunov coefficient
l_{1}=\frac{1}{\omega_{0}}\Re{(c_{1}(0))}
can be derived from the
w^{2}
,
w\bar{w}
and
w^{2}\bar{w}
terms in the homological equation. These terms yield the equations

\displaystyle H_{2000}

\displaystyle=(2i\omega_{0}I_{n}-A)^{-1}B(q,q),

(59)

\displaystyle H_{1100}

\displaystyle=-A^{-1}B(q,\bar{q}),

(60)

\displaystyle(i\omega_{0}I_{n}-A)H_{2100}

\displaystyle=2B(q,H_{1100})+B(\bar{q},H_{2000})+C(q,q,\bar{q})-2c_{1}(0)q.

(61)

Since the last equation is singular, we can use the Fredholm solvability condition to find

c_{1}(0)=\frac{1}{2}\bar{p}^{T}[2B(q,H_{1100})+B(\bar{q},H_{2000})+C(q,q,\bar{q})].

(62)

The vector
H_{2100}
satisfying
\bar{p}^{T}H_{2100}=0
can be found by solving the corresponding bordered matrix system, i.e.

H_{2100}=A^{INV}_{i\omega_{0}}\left(2B(q,H_{1100})+B(\bar{q},H_{2000})+C(q,q,\bar{q})-2c_{1}(0)q\right),

(63)

where we use the shorthand notation
A^{INV}_{i\omega_{0}}=(i\omega_{0}I_{n}-A)^{INV}
. The vector
w=A^{INV}_{i\omega_{0}}v
can be found by solving the following non-singular bordered system

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}i\omega_{0}I_{n}-A&q\\
\bar{p}^{T}&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}w\\
s\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}v\\
0\end{pmatrix},

(64)

for unknown
(w,s)\in\mathbb{C}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
. If
\bar{p}^{T}v=0
, then
s=0
and
w=A^{INV}_{i\omega_{0}}v
will be a solution to the system
(i\omega_{0}I_{n}-A)w=v
satisfying
\bar{p}^{T}w=0
.

For the second Lyapunov coefficient
l_{2}=\frac{1}{\omega_{0}}\Re{(c_{2}(0))}
we need the
w^{3}
,
w^{3}\bar{w}
,
w^{2}\bar{w}^{2}
and
w^{3}\bar{w}^{2}
terms from the homological equation. The first three terms yield the equations

\displaystyle H_{3000}

\displaystyle=(3i\omega_{0}I_{n}-A)^{-1}[3B(q,H_{2000})+C(q,q,q)],

(65)

\displaystyle H_{3100}

\displaystyle=(2i\omega_{0}I_{n}-A)^{-1}[3B(q,H_{2100})+B(\bar{q},H_{3000})+3B(H_{1100},H_{2000})

\displaystyle+3C(q,q,H_{1100})+3C(q,\bar{q},H_{2000})+D(q,q,q,\bar{q})-6c_{1}(0)H_{2000}],

(66)

\displaystyle H_{2200}

\displaystyle=-A^{-1}[2B(q,\overline{H}_{2100})+2B(\bar{q},H_{2100})+B(H_{2000},\overline{H}_{2000})

\displaystyle+2B(H_{1100},H_{1100})+C(q,q,\overline{H}_{2000})+4C(q,\bar{q},H_{1100})

\displaystyle+C(\bar{q},\bar{q},H_{2000})+D(q,q,\bar{q},\bar{q})].

(67)

Note that we used that
c_{1}(0)+\bar{c}_{1}(0)=2\omega_{0}l_{1}=0
to simplify the expression for
H_{2200}
. Collecting the
w^{3}\bar{w}^{2}
terms yields the equation

\displaystyle(i\omega_{0}I_{n}-A)H_{3200}

\displaystyle=M_{3200}-[12c_{2}(0)q+6i\Im{(c_{1}(0))}H_{2100}],

(68)

where

\displaystyle M_{3200}

\displaystyle=3B(q,H_{2200})+2B(\bar{q},H_{3100})+B(\overline{H}_{2000},H_{3000})+6B(H_{1100},H_{2100})

\displaystyle+3B(\overline{H}_{2100},H_{2000})+3C(q,q,\overline{H}_{2100})+6C(q,\bar{q},H_{2100})

\displaystyle+3C(q,H_{2000},\overline{H}_{2000})+6C(q,H_{1100},H_{1100})+C(\bar{q},\bar{q},H_{3000})

\displaystyle+6C(\bar{q},H_{1100},H_{2000})+D(q,q,q,\overline{H}_{2000})+6D(q,q,\bar{q},H_{1100})

\displaystyle+3D(q,\bar{q},\bar{q},H_{2000})+E(q,q,q,\bar{q},\bar{q}).

By applying the Fredholm solvability condition to equation
Eq.

68
, we find that

\displaystyle c_{2}(0)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{12}\bar{p}^{T}M_{3200}.

(69)

For the higher order coefficients we will also need
H_{3200}
. The unique solution to equation
Eq.

68
satisfying
\bar{p}^{T}H_{3200}=0
can again be found by solving the corresponding bordered system:

H_{3200}=A_{i\omega_{0}}^{INV}(M_{3200}-[12c_{2}(0)q+6i\Im{(c_{1}(0))}H_{2100}]).

(70)

An expression of the seventh order coefficient
c_{3}(0)
has been derived in
[
18
]
, using the same normalization method. For this we need the coefficients of the terms
w^{4},w^{4}\bar{w},w^{4}\bar{w}^{2}
and
w^{3}\bar{w}^{3}
from the homological equation. These yield regular systems and the solutions are respectively

\displaystyle H_{4000}

\displaystyle=(4i\omega_{0}I_{n}-A)^{-1}[4B(q,H_{3000})+3B(H_{2000},H_{2000})

\displaystyle+6C(q,q,H_{2000})+D(q,q,q,q)],

(71)

\displaystyle H_{4100}

\displaystyle=(3i\omega_{0}I_{n}-A)^{-1}[4B(q,H_{3100})+B(\bar{q},H_{4000})+4B(H_{1100},H_{3000})

\displaystyle+6B(H_{2000},H_{2100})+6C(q,q,H_{2100})+4C(q,\bar{q},H_{3000})

\displaystyle+12C(q,H_{1100},H_{2000})+3C(\bar{q},H_{2000},H_{2000})+4D(q,q,q,H_{1100})

\displaystyle+6D(q,q,\bar{q},H_{2000})+E(q,q,q,q,\bar{q})-12c_{1}(0)H_{3000}],

(72)

\displaystyle H_{4200}

\displaystyle=(2i\omega_{0}I_{n}-A)^{-1}[4B(q,H_{3200})+2B(\bar{q},H_{4100})

\displaystyle+B(\overline{H}_{2000},H_{4000})+8B(H_{1100},H_{3100})+4B(\overline{H}_{2100},H_{3000})

\displaystyle+6B(H_{2000},H_{2200})+6B(H_{2100},H_{2100})+6C(q,q,H_{2200})

\displaystyle+8C(q,\bar{q},H_{3100})+4C(q,\overline{H}_{2000},H_{3000})+24C(q,H_{1100},H_{2100})

\displaystyle+12C(q,\overline{H}_{2100},H_{2000})+C(\bar{q},\bar{q},H_{4000})+8C(\bar{q},H_{1100},H_{3000})

\displaystyle+12C(\bar{q},H_{2000},H_{2100})+3C(\overline{H}_{2000},H_{2000},H_{2000})+12C(H_{1100},H_{1100},H_{2000})

\displaystyle+4D(q,q,q,\overline{H}_{2100})+12D(q,q,\bar{q},H_{2100})+6D(q,q,\overline{H}_{2000},H_{2000})

\displaystyle+12D(q,q,H_{1100},H_{1100})+4D(q,\bar{q},\bar{q},H_{3000})+24D(q,\bar{q},H_{1100},H_{2000})

\displaystyle+3D(\bar{q},\bar{q},H_{2000},H_{2000})+E(q,q,q,q,\overline{H}_{2000})+8E(q,q,q,\bar{q},H_{1100})

\displaystyle+6E(q,q,\bar{q},\bar{q},H_{2000})+K(q,q,q,q,\bar{q},\bar{q})-8(6c_{2}(0)H_{2000}+2c_{1}(0)H_{3100})],

(73)

\displaystyle H_{3300}

\displaystyle=-A^{-1}[3B(q,\overline{H}_{3200})+3B(\bar{q},H_{3200})+3B(\overline{H}_{2000},H_{3100})+B(\overline{H}_{3000},H_{3000})

\displaystyle+9B(H_{1100},H_{2200})+9B(H_{2100},\overline{H}_{2100})+3B(\overline{H}_{3100},H_{2000})

\displaystyle+3C(q,q,\overline{H}_{3100})+9C(q,\bar{q},H_{2200})+9C(q,\overline{H}_{2000},H_{2100})

\displaystyle+3C(q,\overline{H}_{3000},H_{2000})+18C(q,H_{1100},\overline{H}_{2100})+3C(\bar{q},\bar{q},H_{3100})

\displaystyle+3C(\bar{q},\overline{H}_{2000},H_{3000})+18C(\bar{q},H_{1100},H_{2100})+9C(\bar{q},\overline{H}_{2100},H_{2000})

\displaystyle+9C(\overline{H}_{2000},H_{1100},H_{2000})+6C(H_{1100},H_{1100},H_{1100})+D(q,q,q,\overline{H}_{3000})

\displaystyle+9D(q,q,\bar{q},\overline{H}_{2100})+9D(q,q,\overline{H}_{2000},H_{1100})+9D(q,\bar{q},\bar{q},H_{2100})

\displaystyle+9D(q,\bar{q},\overline{H}_{2000},H_{2000})+18D(q,\bar{q},H_{1100},H_{1100})+D(\bar{q},\bar{q},\bar{q},H_{3000})

\displaystyle+9D(\bar{q},\bar{q},H_{1100},H_{2000})+3E(q,q,q,\bar{q},\overline{H}_{2000})+9E(q,q,\bar{q},\bar{q},H_{1100})

\displaystyle+3E(q,\bar{q},\bar{q},\bar{q},H_{2000})+K(q,q,q,\bar{q},\bar{q},\bar{q})-72d_{2}H_{1100}].

(74)

Here we used that
c_{1}(0)+\overline{c}_{1}(0)=0
and
c_{2}(0)+\overline{c}_{2}(0)=\Re{}(c_{2}(0))=d_{2}
to simplify the last two expressions. Note that the term
D(\bar{q},\bar{q},\bar{q},H_{3000})
in the expression for
H_{3300}
is missing in
[
18
]
, although it was included in their calculations. Collecting the
w^{4}\bar{w}^{3}
terms results in the equation

\displaystyle(i\omega_{0}I_{n}-A)H_{4300}

\displaystyle=M_{4300}-(144c_{3}(0)q+72(2c_{2}(0)+\overline{c}_{2}(0))H_{2100}

\displaystyle+12i\Im{}\{c_{1}(0)\}H_{3200}),

(75)

where we used that
3c_{1}(0)+2\overline{c}_{1}(0)=i\Im{}\{c_{1}(0)\}
. The term
M_{4300}
contains all the derivatives and is presented in the supplementary material (SM1.1).

To obtain the equation for
c_{3}(0)
we apply the Fredholm solvability condition to the singular system
Section

4.1
. This yields the equation

\displaystyle c_{3}(0)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{144}\bar{p}^{T}M_{4300},

where we used that
\bar{p}^{T}H_{2100}=0
and
\bar{p}^{T}H_{3200}=0
. The unique solution to equation
Section

4.1
satisfying
\bar{p}^{T}H_{4300}=0
can again be found by solving the corresponding bordered system:

H_{4300}=A_{i\omega_{0}}^{INV}(M_{4300}-[144c_{3}(0)q+72(2c_{2}(0)+\overline{c}_{2}(0))H_{2100}+12i\Im{}\{c_{1}(0)\}H_{3200}]).

(76)

4.2
Parameter-related coefficients

Linear coefficients
K_{10}
,
K_{01}

We first determine the coefficients for the linear approximation of the parameter transformation
K
. Collecting the
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
terms in
Eq.

52
yields for
\mu=(10),(01)
the systems

\displaystyle AH_{00\mu}=-J_{1}K_{\mu}.

(77)

Let
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
be the standard basis vectors. Then we can write

K_{\mu}=\gamma_{1,\mu}e_{1}+\gamma_{2,\mu}e_{2},

(78)

for some yet unknown
\gamma_{1,\mu},\gamma_{2,\mu}\in\mathbb{R}
. Since
A
is regular, we have

H_{00\mu}=-\gamma_{1,\mu}A^{-1}J_{1}e_{1}-\gamma_{2,\mu}A^{-1}J_{1}e_{2}.

(79)

In the equations that follow we will occasionally use the following notation

\delta_{\mu}^{ij}=\begin{cases}1,\quad\textrm{if }\mu=(ij),\\
0,\quad\textrm{if }\mu=(ji)\end{cases},\quad\textrm{ for }i,j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

(80)

The
\beta_{1}w
and
\beta_{2}w
terms yield the systems

\displaystyle(i\omega_{0}I_{n}-A)H_{10\mu}=A_{1}(q,K_{\mu})+B(q,H_{00\mu})-(\delta_{\mu}^{10}+ib_{1,\mu})q.

(81)

To reduce the length of the equations, it is convenient to define

\Gamma_{i}(q)=A_{1}(q,e_{i})+B(q,-A^{-1}J_{1}e_{i}).

(82)

Substituting equations
Eq.

78
and
Eq.

79
into equation
Eq.

81
results in

\displaystyle(i\omega_{0}I_{n}-A)H_{10\mu}=\gamma_{1,\mu}\Gamma_{1}(q)+\gamma_{2,\mu}\Gamma_{2}(q)-(\delta_{\mu}^{10}+ib_{1,\mu})q,

(83)

Applying the Fredholm solvability condition to equation
Eq.

83
results in

\delta_{\mu}^{10}+ib_{1,\mu}=\bar{p}^{T}[\gamma_{1,\mu}\Gamma_{1}(q)+\gamma_{2,\mu}\Gamma_{2}(q)].

(84)

If we take the real and imaginary parts of the above equation, we find that

\delta_{\mu}^{10}=\gamma_{1,\mu}\Re{[\bar{p}^{T}\Gamma_{1}(q)]}+\gamma_{2,\mu}\Re{[\bar{p}^{T}\Gamma_{2}(q)]},

(85)

and

b_{1,\mu}=\gamma_{1,\mu}\Im{[\bar{p}^{T}\Gamma_{1}(q)]}+\gamma_{2,\mu}\Im{[\bar{p}^{T}\Gamma_{2}(q)]}.

(86)

A solution
H_{10\mu}
of equation
Eq.

83
satisfying
\bar{p}^{T}H_{10\mu}=0
can be obtained by solving the bordered system

\begin{pmatrix}i\omega_{0}I_{n}-A&q\\
\bar{p}^{T}&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}H_{10\mu}\\
s\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\gamma_{1,\mu}\Gamma_{1}(q)+\gamma_{2,\mu}\Gamma_{2}(q)-(\delta_{\mu}^{10}+ib_{1,\mu})q\\
0\end{pmatrix}.

Since the coefficients
b_{1,\mu}
,
\gamma_{1,\mu}
and
\gamma_{2,\mu}
are still unknown, this system cannot be solved directly. Instead, we derive another equation similar to
Eq.

85
, where the only unknown are
\gamma_{1,\mu}
and
\gamma_{2,\mu}
. This will then allow us to set up a linear system to solve for these coefficients. To achieve this, we would like to apply the bordered inverse linearly to the separate terms. However, applied to the separate terms, the solution to the bordered system
Eq.

64
may not satisfy
s=0
, losing its original interpretation. We write
w=A_{i\omega_{0}}^{Inv}v
when
w
is obtained by solving system
Eq.

64
without ensuring
s=0
, i.e.
w=A_{i\omega_{0}}^{Inv}v
does not necessarily solve
(i\omega_{0}I_{n}-A)w=v
. The solution of equation
Eq.

83
can then be written as

H_{10\mu}=\gamma_{1,\mu}A^{Inv}_{i\omega_{0}}\Gamma_{1}(q)+\gamma_{2,\mu}A^{Inv}_{i\omega_{0}}\Gamma_{2}(q)-(\delta_{\mu}^{10}+ib_{1,\mu})A^{Inv}_{i\omega_{0}}q.

(87)

Collecting the
w^{2}\beta_{i}
and
w\bar{w}\beta_{i}
terms respectively yield the systems

\displaystyle(2i\omega_{0}I_{n}-A)H_{20\mu}

\displaystyle=A_{1}(H_{2000},K_{\mu})+2B(q,H_{10\mu})+B(H_{00\mu},H_{2000})

\displaystyle+B_{1}(q,q,K_{\mu})+C(q,q,H_{00\mu})-2(\delta_{\mu}^{10}+ib_{1,\mu})H_{2000},

\displaystyle-AH_{11\mu}

\displaystyle=A_{1}(H_{1100},K_{\mu})+2\Re{\{B(\bar{q},H_{10\mu})\}}+B(H_{00\mu},H_{1100})

\displaystyle+B_{1}(q,\bar{q},K_{\mu})+C(q,\bar{q},H_{00\mu})-2\delta_{\mu}^{10}H_{1100}.

Both these systems are regular with solutions

\displaystyle H_{20\mu}

\displaystyle=A_{2i\omega_{0}}^{-1}[A_{1}(H_{2000},K_{\mu})+2B(q,H_{10\mu})+B(H_{00\mu},H_{2000})
