Remark

The equations for the parameter-dependent coefficients can be simplified by noting that
A^{Inv}_{i\omega_{0}}q=0
. To see this, write the corresponding bordered system as the following system of equations

\displaystyle(i\omega_{0}I_{n}-A)w+qs

\displaystyle=q,

\displaystyle\bar{p}^{T}w=0.

Taking the inner product with
p
in the first equation yields
s=1
. Then the first equation becomes
(i\omega_{0}I_{n}-A)w=0
, with solution
w=\lambda q
. Filling this into the second equation yields
\bar{p}^{T}w=\lambda\bar{p}^{T}q=0
from which it follows that
\lambda=0
. Thus, the unique solution to this system is
(w,s)=(0,1)
. As a result, all terms containing
A^{Inv}_{i\omega_{0}}q
vanish. Nevertheless, we included them here as this will make the conversion to DDEs in the following section easier.

5
Coefficients of the parameter-dependent normal form and the predictor in DDEs

Assume that system
Eq.

10
has an equilibrium at the origin at
\alpha=(0,0)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
with only one pair of purely imaginary simple eigenvalues

\lambda_{1,2}=\pm i\omega_{0},\quad\omega_{0}>0,

and no other eigenvalues on the imaginary axis. Furthermore, we assume that the first Lyapunov coefficient
l_{1}(0)=0
and the second Lyapunov coefficient
l_{2}(0)\neq 0
.
We have eigenfunctions
\varphi
and
\varphi^{\odot}
satisfying

A\varphi=i\omega_{0}\varphi,\quad A^{\star}\varphi^{\odot}=i\omega_{0}\varphi^{\odot},\quad\langle\varphi^{\odot},\varphi\rangle=1,

where
\langle\varphi^{\odot},\varphi\rangle
is given by the pairing
Eq.

14
.
Furthermore, introduce
q,p\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
such that

\Delta(i\omega_{0})q=0,\quad p^{T}\Delta(i\omega_{0})=0,\quad p^{T}\Delta^{\prime}(i\omega_{0})q=1.

With
q
and
p
as above, explicit expressions for the eigenfunctions
\varphi
and
\varphi^{\odot}
are respectively given by equations
Eq.

27
and
Eq.

28
. Points
y\in X_{0}
of the real critical eigenspace can be represented in terms of the complex coordinate
z=\langle\varphi^{\odot},y\rangle
as,

y=z\varphi+\bar{z}\bar{\varphi}.

The homological equation
Eq.

22
becomes

\displaystyle A^{\odot\star}jH(z,\bar{z},\beta)+J_{1}K(\beta)r^{\odot\star}+

\displaystyle R(H(z,\bar{z},\beta),K(\beta))

\displaystyle=j(D_{z}H(z,\bar{z},\beta)\dot{z}+D_{\bar{z}}H(z,\bar{z},\beta)\dot{\bar{z}}).

(117)

Restricted to the two-dimensional center manifold, the system can be transformed into the same normalform
Section

4
with new unfolding parameters
\beta=(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
if the transversality condition
Eq.

39
holds. Thus, we take the same truncated normal form:

\displaystyle\dot{z}

\displaystyle=(i\omega_{0}+\beta_{1}+ib_{1}(\beta))z+(\beta_{2}+ib_{2}(\beta))z|z|^{2}+(c_{2}(0)+g_{3201}\beta_{2})z|z|^{4}

\displaystyle+c_{3}(0)z|z|^{6},

(118)

where
b_{1}(\beta)
and
\beta_{2}(\beta)
are expanded as
Eq.

54
and
Eq.

55
.
The nonlinearity in the homological equation
Section

5
is expanded as

\displaystyle R(u,\alpha)

\displaystyle=[A_{1}(u,\alpha)+\frac{1}{2}B(u,u)+\frac{1}{2}J_{2}(\alpha,\alpha)+\frac{1}{6}C(u,u,u)+\frac{1}{2}B_{1}(u,u,\alpha)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}A_{2}(u,\alpha,\alpha)+\frac{1}{6}J_{3}(\alpha,\alpha,\alpha)+\frac{1}{24}D(u,u,u,u)+\frac{1}{6}C_{1}(u,u,u,\alpha)+\frac{1}{4}B_{2}(u,u,\alpha,\alpha)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{6}A_{3}(u,\alpha,\alpha,\alpha)+\frac{1}{120}E(u,u,u,u,u)+\frac{1}{24}D_{1}(u,u,u,u,\alpha)+\frac{1}{12}C_{2}(u,u,u,\alpha,\alpha)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{12}B_{3}(u,u,\alpha,\alpha,\alpha)+\frac{1}{720}K(u,u,u,u,u,u)+\frac{1}{120}E_{1}(u,u,u,u,u,\alpha)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{36}C_{3}(u,u,u,\alpha,\alpha,\alpha)+\frac{1}{5040}L(u,u,u,u,u,u,u)]r^{\odot\star}.

Finally,
H
is expanded as

\displaystyle H(z,\bar{z},\beta)

\displaystyle=z\varphi+\bar{z}\bar{\varphi}+\sum_{n+m=2}^{7}\frac{1}{n!m!}H_{nm00}z^{n}\bar{z}^{m}+\sum_{n+m=0}^{5}H_{nm01}\frac{1}{n!m!}z^{n}\bar{z}^{m}\beta_{2}

\displaystyle+\sum_{n+m=0}^{3}\frac{1}{n!m!}H_{nm10}z^{n}\bar{z}^{m}\beta_{1}+\sum_{n+m=0}^{3}\frac{1}{2n!m!}H_{nm02}z^{n}\bar{z}^{m}\beta_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle+\sum_{n+m=0}^{1}\frac{1}{n!m!}H_{nm11}z^{n}\bar{z}^{m}\beta_{1}\beta_{2}+\sum_{n+m=0}^{1}\frac{1}{6n!m!}H_{nm03}z^{n}\bar{z}^{m}\beta_{2}^{3}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}+\frac{1}{6}H_{1103}z\bar{z}\beta_{2}^{3}+\frac{1}{12}H_{2003}z^{2}\beta_{2}^{3}+\frac{1}{12}H_{2103}z^{2}\bar{z}\beta_{2}^{3}}.

(119)

Note that
H
is now a mapping into
X=C([-h,0],\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Since
X
is real, the property
H_{ijkl}=\overline{H}_{jikl}
still holds. Additionally, the parameter relation
K
remains the same as in the ODE case, given by
Eq.

58
.

The equations collected from the homological equation
Section

5
have a similar structure as those in the ODE case. To obtain the corresponding equations for DDEs, one substitutes
A
with
A^{\odot\star}
,
q
with
\varphi
, appends
r^{\odot\star}
after every derivative and adds a
j
in front of the remaining terms. Despite these similarities, the solutions differ significantly as we now solve linear operator equations on
X^{\odot\star}
.

In the following subsection, we will illustrate how the equations for the coefficients change and how each case can be solved using the results presented in
Section

2.2.2
. We focus on presenting the critical normal form coefficients, as the parameter-dependent equations are solved similarly. Full derivations of the parameter-dependent equations are presented in the supplementary material (S1.2).

5.1
Critical normal form coefficients

For the computation of the critical normal form coefficients up to
c_{2}(0)
we follow
[
14
]
. Collecting the
z^{2},z\bar{z}
and
z^{3}
terms from the homological equation
Section

5
results in the following linear systems

\displaystyle(2i\omega_{0}I_{n}-A^{\odot\star})jH_{2000}

\displaystyle=B(\varphi,\varphi)r^{\odot\star},

\displaystyle-A^{\odot\star}jH_{1100}

\displaystyle=B(\varphi,\bar{\varphi})r^{\odot\star},

\displaystyle(3i\omega_{0}I_{n}-A^{\odot\star})jH_{3000}

\displaystyle=[3B(\varphi,H_{2000})+C(\varphi,\varphi,\varphi)]r^{\odot\star}.

All three equations are regular with a right-hand side of the form
(w_{0},w)=(w_{0},0)
. Thus, applying
Section

2.2.2
.1 with
\eta=0
results in the following solutions

\displaystyle H_{2000}(\theta)

\displaystyle=e^{2i\omega_{0}\theta}\Delta^{-1}(2i\omega_{0})B(\varphi,\varphi),

(120)

\displaystyle H_{1100}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\Delta^{-1}(0)B(\varphi,\bar{\varphi}),

(121)

\displaystyle H_{3000}(\theta)

\displaystyle=e^{3i\omega_{0}\theta}\Delta^{-1}(3i\omega_{0})[3B(\varphi,H_{2000})+C(\varphi,\varphi,\varphi)].

(122)

Collecting the
z^{2}\bar{z}
terms yields the following singular system

\displaystyle(i\omega_{0}I_{n}-A^{\odot\star})jH_{2100}

\displaystyle=[2B(\varphi,H_{1100})+B(\bar{\varphi},H_{2000})+C(\varphi,\varphi,\bar{\varphi})]r^{\odot\star}-2c_{1}(0)j\varphi.

Applying the Fredhom solvability condition to the above system results in

\displaystyle c_{1}(0)=\frac{1}{2}\langle[2B(\varphi,H_{1100})+B(\bar{\varphi},H_{2000})+C(\varphi,\varphi,\bar{\varphi})]r^{\odot\star},\varphi^{\odot}\rangle.

Here we used that
\langle j\varphi,\varphi^{\odot}\rangle=\langle\varphi^{\odot},\varphi\rangle=1
which follows from the pairings
Eq.

14
and
Eq.

17
. We can evaluate the above pairing using equation
Eq.

17
and the expression
Eq.

28
for
\varphi^{\odot}
. This results in the following formula

c_{1}(0)=\frac{1}{2}p^{T}[2B(\varphi,H_{1100})+B(\bar{\varphi},H_{2000})+C(\varphi,\varphi,\bar{\varphi})].

Finally, to obtain the expression for
H_{2100}(\theta)
we apply
Section

2.2.2
to obtain the unique solution

H_{2100}(\theta)=B_{i\omega_{0}}^{INV}(2B(\varphi,H_{1100})+B(\bar{\varphi},H_{2000})+C(\varphi,\varphi,\bar{\varphi}),-2c_{1}(0)q)(\theta)

satisfying
\langle\varphi^{\odot},H_{2100}\rangle=0.
Henceforth, we denote the linear combination of multilinear forms as
M_{ijkl}
, where
M_{ijkl}
is defined as in the ODE case but with all vectors
q
replaced by functions
\varphi
. Similarly to the first three systems we find for
H_{2200}
the equation

\displaystyle H_{2200}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\Delta^{-1}(0)M_{2200}.

(123)

The
z^{3}\bar{z}
terms yield the linear system

\displaystyle(2i\omega_{0}I-A^{\odot\star})jH_{3100}

\displaystyle=M_{3100}r^{\odot\star}-6c_{1}(0)jH_{2000}.

Notice that the right hand side is of the form
(w_{0},w)
with
w_{0}=M_{3100}-6c_{1}(0)H_{2000}(0)
and

w(\theta)=-6c_{1}(0)H_{2000}(\theta)=e^{2i\omega_{0}\theta}\Delta^{-1}(2i\omega_{0})(-6c_{1}(0)B(\varphi,\varphi)).

Thus, we can apply
Section

2.2.2
with
\eta=-6c_{1}(0)B(\varphi,\varphi)
and
\xi_{1}=\xi_{2}=0
, which yields

\displaystyle H_{3100}(\theta)

\displaystyle=e^{2i\omega_{0}\theta}\Delta^{-1}(2i\omega_{0})M_{3100}-6c_{1}(0)\Delta^{-1}(2i\omega_{0})[\Delta^{\prime}(2i\omega_{0})-\theta\Delta(2i\omega_{0})]H_{2000}(\theta).

(124)

Collecting the
z^{3}\bar{z}^{2}
terms results in the equation

\displaystyle(i\omega_{0}I_{n}-A^{\odot\star})jH_{3200}

\displaystyle=M_{3200}r^{\odot\star}-12c_{2}(0)j\varphi-6i\Im{\{c_{1}(0)\}}jH_{2100}.

(125)

Applying the Fredholm solvability condition gives

\displaystyle c_{2}(0)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{12}p^{T}M_{3200}.

We proceed by deriving the coefficients needed to compute the seventh-order coefficient
c_{3}(0)
. We can now apply
Section

2.2.2
to find the solution

\displaystyle H_{3200}(\theta)=B_{i\omega_{0}}^{INV}\left(M_{3200},-12c_{2}(0)q-6i\Im\{c_{1}(0)\}H_{2100}(0),-12i\Im\{c_{1}(0)\}c_{1}(0)q\right)(\theta),

satisfying
\langle\varphi^{\odot},H_{3200}\rangle=0.
Solving for the expressions of the coefficients
H_{4000},H_{4100}
and
H_{3300}
is similar to how we solved for
H_{3100}
with the application of
Section

2.2.2
. This results in the following expressions

\displaystyle H_{4000}(\theta)

\displaystyle=e^{4i\omega_{0}\theta}\Delta^{-1}(4i\omega_{0})M_{4000},

(126)

\displaystyle H_{4100}(\theta)

\displaystyle=e^{3i\omega_{0}\theta}\Delta^{-1}(3i\omega_{0})M_{4100}-12c_{1}(0)\Delta^{-1}(3i\omega_{0})[\Delta^{\prime}(3i\omega_{0})-\theta\Delta(3i\omega_{0})]H_{3000}(\theta),

(127)

and

\displaystyle H_{3300}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\Delta^{-1}(0)M_{3300}-72d_{2}\Delta^{-1}(0)[\Delta^{\prime}(0)-\theta\Delta(0)]H_{1100}(\theta).

(128)

The equation for
H_{4200}
is of the following form

\displaystyle(2i\omega_{0}I_{n}-A^{\odot\star})jH_{4200}

\displaystyle=M_{4200}r^{\odot\star}-48c_{2}(0)jH_{2000}-16c_{1}(0)jH_{3100},

(129)

Using the linearity of the inverse operator, we can apply
Section

2.2.2
for the first two terms. Then to determine the inverse of the last term, we apply
Section

2.2.2
with
M=M_{3100}
,
\hat{\eta}=-6c_{1}(0)H_{2000}(0)
and
\hat{\xi}=0
. This results in the following expression

\displaystyle H_{4200}(\theta)

\displaystyle=e^{2i\omega_{0}\theta}\Delta^{-1}(2i\omega_{0})M_{4200}-48c_{2}(0)\Delta^{-1}(2i\omega_{0})[\Delta^{\prime}(2i\omega_{0})-\theta\Delta(2i\omega_{0})]H_{2000}(\theta)

\displaystyle-16c_{1}(0)e^{2i\omega_{0}\theta}\Delta^{-1}(2i\omega_{0})\Bigl([\Delta^{\prime}(2i\omega_{0})-\theta\Delta(2i\omega_{0})]H_{3100}(0)

\displaystyle+3c_{1}(0)[\Delta^{\prime\prime}(2i\omega_{0})-\theta^{2}\Delta(2i\omega_{0})]H_{2000}(0)\Bigr).

(130)

The third Lyapunov coefficient is derived from the system

\displaystyle(i\omega_{0}I_{n}-A^{\odot\star})jH_{4300}

\displaystyle=M_{4300}r^{\odot\star}-(144c_{3}(0)j\varphi+72(2c_{2}(0)+\overline{c_{2}}(0))jH_{2100}

\displaystyle+12i\Im{}\{c_{1}(0)\}jH_{3200}),

(131)

Applying the Fredholm solvability condition to equation
Section

5.1
will then result in

c_{3}(0)=\frac{1}{144}p^{T}M_{4300},

where we used that
\langle\varphi^{\odot},H_{2100}\rangle=0
and
\langle\varphi^{\odot},H_{3200}\rangle=0
.

5.2
Parameter-dependent coefficients

To arrive at the equations for the unknown coefficients in the parameter transformation
K
, we can follow the same procedure as in
Section

4.2
with the new equations. The complete derivation is shown in the supplementary material (SM2). The resulting systems remain very similar to the systems
Eq.

94
. After changing all
q
to
\varphi
and
\bar{p}
to
p
one essentially only needs to determine the correct representations for all terms with an inverse matrix operator in front of it. Nevertheless, one needs to be careful. For instance, after defining
\Gamma_{i}(\varphi)=A_{1}(\varphi,e_{i})+B(\varphi,\Delta^{-1}(0)J_{1}e_{i})
, equation
Eq.

83
for the coefficients
H_{10\mu}
will become

\displaystyle(i\omega_{0}I_{n}-A^{\odot\star})jH_{10\mu}=[\gamma_{1,\mu}\Gamma_{1}(\varphi)+\gamma_{2,\mu}\Gamma_{2}(\varphi)]r^{\odot\star}-(\delta_{\mu}^{10}+ib_{1,\mu})j\varphi.

(132)

A solution
H_{10\mu}
of equation
Eq.

132
satisfying
\langle\varphi^{\odot},H_{10\mu}\rangle=0
can be obtained with
Section

2.2.2
:

\displaystyle H_{10\mu}(\theta)=B_{i\omega_{0}}^{INV}(\gamma_{1,\mu}\Gamma_{1}(\varphi)+\gamma_{2,\mu}\Gamma_{2}(\varphi),-(\delta_{\mu}^{10}+ib_{1,\mu})q)(\theta)

(133)

To free the unknown coefficients
\gamma_{1,\mu}
and
\gamma_{2,\mu}
we need to rewrite the equation. As with ODEs, we can apply the bordered inverse to the separate terms but this loses the original interpretation. Thus, we write
B^{Inv}_{i\omega_{0}}
whenever only the notation from
Section

2.2.2
is used, i.e.
v=B^{Inv}_{i\omega_{0}}(\eta,\xi_{0},\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{m})
is defined by equation
Eq.

38
from
Section

2.2.2
but does not nevessarily solve
(i\omega_{0}I-A^{\odot\star})jv=jw
. With this we can rewrite (
133
) as

H_{10\mu}=\gamma_{1,\mu}B^{Inv}_{i\omega_{0}}(\Gamma_{1}(\varphi))+\gamma_{2,\mu}B^{Inv}_{i\omega_{0}}(\Gamma_{2}(\varphi))-(\delta_{\mu}^{10}+ib_{1,\mu})B^{Inv}_{i\omega_{0}}(0,q).

(134)

The important thing is that with all three terms combined,
Eq.

134
gives us the same expression as
Eq.

133
which solves equation
Eq.

132
.
As an example, the system that solves for the coefficients
K_{10}
and
K_{01}
will have the following form:

P\begin{pmatrix}\gamma_{1,\mu}\\
\gamma_{2,\mu}\end{pmatrix}=Q_{\mu},

(135)

where
P\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
has for
k=1,2
the compenents

\displaystyle P_{1k}

\displaystyle=\Re{[p^{T}\Gamma_{k}(\varphi)]},

(136)

\displaystyle P_{2k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Re{}\Bigl\{p^{T}\Bigl[\Gamma_{k}(H_{2100})+2B\left(\varphi,\Delta^{-1}(0)\Pi_{k}(H_{1100},\bar{\varphi},\varphi)\right)

\displaystyle+B\left(\bar{\varphi},e^{2i\omega_{0}\theta}\Delta^{-1}(2i\omega_{0})\Lambda_{k}(H_{2000},\varphi,\varphi)\right)+B\left(H_{2000},\overline{B_{i\omega_{0}}^{Inv}(\Gamma_{k}(\varphi))}\right)

\displaystyle+2B\left(H_{1100},B_{i\omega_{0}}^{Inv}(\Gamma_{k}(\varphi))\right)+2B_{1}(\varphi,H_{1100},e_{k})

\displaystyle+B_{1}(\bar{\varphi},H_{2000},e_{k})+C\left(\varphi,\varphi,\overline{B_{i\omega_{0}}^{Inv}(\Gamma_{k}(\varphi))}\right)+2C\left(\varphi,\bar{\varphi},B_{i\omega_{0}}^{Inv}(\Gamma_{k}(\varphi))\right)

\displaystyle+2C\left(\varphi,H_{1100},\Delta^{-1}(0)J_{1}e_{k}\right)+C(\bar{\varphi},H_{2000},\Delta^{-1}(0)J_{1}e_{k})+C_{1}(\varphi,\varphi,\bar{\varphi},e_{k})

\displaystyle+D(\varphi,\varphi,\bar{\varphi},\Delta^{-1}(0)J_{1}e_{k})+\Im{[p^{T}\Gamma_{k}(\varphi)]}\Bigl(-4B\left(\varphi,\Delta^{-1}(0)\Re{[iB\left(\bar{\varphi},B_{i\omega_{0}}^{Inv}(0,q)\right)]}\right)

\displaystyle-2iB\left(\bar{\varphi},e^{2i\omega_{0}\theta}\Delta^{-1}(2i\omega_{0})\left([\Delta^{\prime}(2i\omega_{0})-\theta\Delta(2i\omega_{0})]H_{2000}(0)+B\left(\varphi,B_{i\omega_{0}}^{Inv}(0,q)\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle\left.\left.\left.+iB\left(H_{2000},\overline{B_{i\omega_{0}}^{Inv}(0,q)}\right)-2iB\left(H_{1100},B_{i\omega_{0}}^{Inv}(0,q)\right)+iC\left(\varphi,\varphi,\overline{B_{i\omega_{0}}^{Inv}(0,q)}\right)\right.\right.\right.

\displaystyle-2iC\left(\varphi,\bar{\varphi},B_{i\omega_{0}}^{Inv}(0,q)\right)\Bigr)\Bigr]\Bigr\},

(137)

and
Q_{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
\mu=(10),(01)
is given by

\displaystyle Q_{1,\mu}

\displaystyle=\delta_{\mu}^{10},

(138)

\displaystyle Q_{2,\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\mu}^{10}\Re{}\Bigl\{p^{T}\Bigl[4B\left(\varphi,\Delta^{-1}(0)\left([\Delta^{\prime}(0)-\theta\Delta(0)]H_{1100}(\theta)+\Re{}\left\{B\left(\bar{\varphi},B_{i\omega_{0}}^{Inv}(0,q)\right)\right\}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+2B\left(\bar{\varphi},e^{2i\omega_{0}\theta}\Delta^{-1}(2i\omega_{0})\left([\Delta^{\prime}(2i\omega_{0})-\theta\Delta(2i\omega_{0})]H_{2000}(0)+B\left(\varphi,B_{i\omega_{0}}^{Inv}(0,q)\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle+B\left(H_{2000},\overline{B_{i\omega_{0}}^{Inv}(0,q)}\right)+2B\left(H_{1100},B_{i\omega_{0}}^{Inv}(0,q)\right)+C\left(\varphi,\varphi,\overline{B_{i\omega_{0}}^{Inv}(0,q)}\right)

\displaystyle+2C\left(\varphi,\bar{\varphi},B_{i\omega_{0}}^{Inv}(0,q)\right)\Bigr]\Bigr\}+\delta_{\mu}^{01}.

(139)

6
The higher order
LPC
predictors

6.1
The predictor for ODEs

The parameter values on the
LPC
curve are approximated as

\displaystyle\beta_{1}=d_{2}\varepsilon^{4}+2(d_{3}-a_{3201}d_{2})\varepsilon^{6},\quad\beta_{2}=-2d_{2}\varepsilon^{2}+(4a_{3201}d_{2}-3d_{3})\varepsilon^{4},

for
\varepsilon>0
.
Taking the expansion
Eq.

58
for
K
, we have the following parameter predictor

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle=\alpha_{0}+K(\beta_{1},\beta_{2}).

(140)

To approximate the solution in phase space we substitute
w=\varepsilon e^{i\psi}
into the expansion
Section

4
of
H
together with the approximations in the
\beta
parameters, where it is not necessary to include the last three terms that are marked grey. Thus, for
\psi\in[0,2\pi]
the periodic orbit can be approximated as

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=x_{0}+H(\varepsilon e^{i\psi},\varepsilon e^{-i\psi},\beta_{1},\beta_{2}).

(141)

Finally, the period is approximated by equation
Section

3.1.2
,

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle=2\pi/(\omega_{0}+(\Im{(c_{1}(0))}-2d_{2}b_{1,01})\varepsilon^{2}+[d_{2}b_{1,10}+(4a_{3201}d_{2}-3d_{3})b_{1,01}+2d_{2}^{2}b_{1,02}

\displaystyle-2d_{2}b_{2,01}+\Im{(c_{2}(0))}]\varepsilon^{4}).

6.2
The predictor for DDEs

The parameter approximations for the
LPC
curve for DDEs remain the same as for ODEs, where the expression for
K
is given by
Eq.

58
and the period is given by
Section

3.1.2
. Similarly, the periodic orbit is approximated for
\psi\in[0,2\pi]
as

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=x_{0}+H(\varepsilon e^{i\psi},\varepsilon e^{-i\psi},\beta_{1},\beta_{2}).

(142)

However, now this defines a function in
C([-h,0],\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Since, we know that the solution that we are looking for is periodic, we have that
x(\theta)=x(\theta+T)
for all
\theta\in[-h,0]
. Thus, it is enough to ensure that
x(0)=x(T)
when solving the system for the localisation of the periodic cycle. Therefore, we take
H_{ijkl}=H_{ijkl}(0)
for the coefficients in the approximation of
H
when we approximate the cycle.

7
Examples

In this section, we demonstrate the performance of the higher-order predictor. We begin with two ODE systems: the Bazykin and Khibnik prey-predator model and the extended Lorenz-84 model. For these examples, we visually compare the numerically computed Limit Point of Cycles curve with both the first-order and higher-order predictors in the parameter space to illustrate the significant improvement in accuracy.

We then conclude with a more challenging DDE system, the FitzHugh-Nagumo neural model with delay. For this case, we also provide a detailed convergence plot, which quantitatively shows that the new method achieves a higher order of convergence, providing robust evidence of its superior performance.

7.1
ODE Examples

We have implemented all the equations for the higher-order predictor in the programming language Julia. For the numerical computation of the generalized Hopf points and the continuation of the
LPC
curves, we used the existing Julia package BifurcationKit
[
20
]
. The option to compute the higher-order predictor has been added to the BifurcationKit package. In the following two examples, we will compare the new higher-order predictor to the first-order predictor from
[
16
]
. Note that in both examples, all derivatives have been computed symbolically using the Symbolic.jl package. We have included a section in the BifurcationKit documentation (cite) that walks through the Lorenz-84 example.

7.1.1
Bazykin and Khibnik prey-predator model

As a first example, we consider a version of a prey-predator system by Bazykin and Khibnik
[
2
]
. The model consists of the following two equations

\begin{cases}\dot{x}&={\displaystyle\frac{x^{2}(1-x)}{n+x}-xy},\\
\dot{y}&=-y(m-x),\end{cases}

(143)

where
x,y\geq 0
and
0<m<1
. One can show analytically that an Andronov-Hopf bifurcation occurs along the curve
n=m^{2}/(1-2m)
. The first Lyapunov coefficient along this curve is positive for
0<m<\frac{1}{4}
and negative for
\frac{1}{4}<m<\frac{1}{2}
, while it vanishes at
(m,n)=(\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{8})
. Thus, a generalized Hopf bifurcation occurs in this system. The bifurcation diagram of
Eq.

143
is shown in
Fig.

3
.

Figure 3:
The bifurcation structure of the Bazykin-Khibnik model
Eq.

143
in the
(m,n)
-parameter plane. The analytical curve of Hopf bifurcations changes stability at the generalized Hopf (GH) point, transitioning between subcritical and supercritical regimes. The GH point also serves as the origin for a curve of Limit Point of Cycles (LPC) bifurcations, which has been continued numerically.

At the generalized Hopf bifurcation we have the eigenvalues
\lambda_{1,2}=\pm i\omega_{0}
, with
\omega_{0}=\sqrt{2}/4
. We take the following eigenvectors

\displaystyle q=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{3}\sqrt{3}\\
-\frac{1}{3}\sqrt{6}i\end{pmatrix},\quad p=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{3}\\
-\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{6}i\end{pmatrix},

satisfying
\bar{q}^{T}q=\bar{p}^{T}q=1
. Using these eigenvectors, the second Lyapunov coefficient has the exact value
1
1

1

This value was calculated in MATLAB using the Symbolic Math Toolbox

l_{2}=-\frac{1024}{729}\sqrt{2}
. In our implementation in Julia, this value is numerically approximated by
l_{2}=-1.986494770740791
.
Figure

4
shows a close-up of the bifurcation diagram, including the first-order and the higher-order predictors in parameter space.

Figure 4:
On the left, the bifurcation diagram near the generalized Hopf bifurcation in the Bazykin-Khibnik Model
Eq.

143
is shown together with the first-order and the higher-order
LPC
predictors. The figure on the right shows the
LPC
curve and the predictors after a translation to the origin and a rotation.

7.1.2
The extended Lorenz-84 model

Our second example is an extended version of the Lorenz-84, which approximates the dynamics of an atmospheric flow model
[
19
]
. In this model,
X
represents the strength of the jet stream, while
Y
and
Z
model the sine and cosine coefficients of the baroclinic wave. In
[
17
]
, the model was extended to include the variable
U
to study the effect of external parameters, such as temperature, on the jet stream and baroclinic waves. The same model was used as an example in
[
16
]
. The extended model consists of four equations:

\begin{cases}\dot{X}&=-Y^{2}-Z^{2}-\alpha X+\alpha F-\gamma U^{2},\\
\dot{Y}&=XY-\beta XZ-Y+G,\\
\dot{Z}&=\beta XY+XZ-Z,\\
\dot{U}&=-\delta U+\gamma UX+T.\end{cases}

(144)

We take
F
and
T
as the bifurcation parameters and fix the parameters

\alpha=0.25,\quad\beta=1,\quad G=0.25,\quad\delta=1.04,\quad\gamma=0.987.

Then, the system has a generalized Hopf bifurcation at
(F,T)\approx(2.3763,0.05019)
. At the generalized Hopf bifurcation, we have the purely imaginary eigenvalues
\lambda=\pm i\omega_{0}
, with
\omega_{0}=0.690367
and we take the eigenvectors such that
\bar{q}^{T}q=\bar{p}^{T}q=1
. The second Lyapunov coefficient is computed as
l_{2}=0.22567
.
Fig.

5
shows the first-order and the higher-order predictors in parameter space next to the numerically continued
LPC
curve.

Figure 5:
The numerically computed
LPC
curve emanating from the generalized Hopf point in the extended Lorenz-84 model
Eq.

144
together with the first-order and higher-order predictors.

7.2
DDE example: Coupled FHN neural system with delay

The numerical computation of the generalized Hopf points and the continuation of the
LPC
curves and the calculation of the higher-order coefficients for the higher-order predictor have been implemented in Julia. The code to produce the numerical results of the example below can be obtained upon request. We compare the higher-order predictor to the first-order predictor from
[
4
]
.

We consider the coupled FitzHugh-Nagumo model from
[
21
]
which was also used to test the predictor in
[
4
]
. This system consists of the following set of equations

\begin{cases}\dot{u}_{1}(t)=-\frac{1}{3}u_{1}^{3}(t)+(c+\alpha)u_{1}^{2}(t)+du_{1}(t)-u_{2}(t)+2\beta f(u_{1}(t-\tau)),\\
\dot{u}_{2}(t)=\varepsilon(u_{1}(t)-bu_{2}(t)).\end{cases}

(145)

In this model,
\alpha
and
\beta
measure the synaptic strength in self-connection and neighbourhood interaction, respectively, and
\tau>0
represents the time delay in signal transmission. The function
f
is a sufficiently smooth sigmoidal amplification function. The parameters
b
and
\varepsilon
are assumed to satisfy
0<b<1
and
0<\varepsilon\ll 1
.

As in
[
21
]
, we take
\beta
and
\alpha
as the bifurcation parameters and fix the parameters

b=0.9,\quad\varepsilon=0.08,\quad c=2.0528,\quad d=-3.2135,\quad\tau=1.7722

Furthermore, we use
f(u)=\tanh(u)
for the sigmoid amplification function. There is a generalized Hopf point at
(\beta,\alpha)=(1.9,-1.0429)
. The left and right vectors null vectors of the characteristic matrix function evaluated at the critical eigenvalues are taken such that
\bar{q}^{T}q=1
and
p^{T}\Delta^{\prime}(i\omega_{0})q=1
. The second Lyapunov coefficient is negative and has the value
l_{2}=-15.6733
.
Figure

6
(a) shows that while the linear predictor is only tangent to the Hopf curve, our higher-order predictor provides a visually excellent approximation, being nearly identical to the computed LPC curve.
Figure

6
(b) quantitatively evaluates the performance of the predictors by plotting their relative error against the step size
\epsilon
on a log-log scale. The relative error is calculated between the approximate solution, as generated by the first- and higher-order predictors, and the “true" solution, which is taken as the result from a Newton-Raphson method converged to a tolerance of 1e-8. The slope of the fitted lines reveals the order of convergence for each method. The analysis demonstrates that the higher-order predictor (red squares) achieves a convergence rate with a slope of approximately
4.64
, more than double the
\approx 2.21
slope of the first-order predictor (blue circles). This confirms that the higher-order method provides a significantly more accurate initial guess, from which the convergence to the true solution branch is much faster.

Figure 6:
Comparison of predictor performance and convergence. (a) The bifurcation diagram near the generalized Hopf bifurcation in the coupled FitzHugh-Nagumo model
Eq.

145
together with first-order and higher-order predictors. (b) Log-log convergence plot showing the relative error of the predictors.

8
Conclusions

In this work, we derived higher-order approximations to the
LPC
curve emanating from the generalized Hopf bifurcation in ordinary and delay differential equations through parameter-dependent center manifold reduction and normalization. This is the only generic codimension-two equilibrium bifurcation where non-hyperbolic limit cycles emanate tangentially to the codimension-one equilibrium bifurcation curve from the bifurcation point. By doing so, we have completed a comprehensive framework for analyzing such bifurcations, providing deeper insights into their dynamics and establishing a solid foundation for future advances in the study of complex dynamical systems.

In addition to presenting a detailed derivation of the parameter-dependent normal form coefficients and transformations, we demonstrated the practical applications and robustness of our method. This was validated through several numerical examples, culminating in a convergence plot for the challenging FitzHugh-Nagumo model with delay, which provides quantitative proof of the predictor’s higher-order accuracy.

The higher-order predictors developed in this work have been implemented and are publicly available on GitHub. Specifically, the method for ODEs is integrated into the Julia package BifurcationKit.jl, while the DDE implementation resides in a custom package. However, the underlying mathematical framework is general and not specific to these Julia implementations. Therefore, it could be readily integrated into other established bifurcation analysis toolboxes to benefit a wider user base. For instance, the ODE predictor could be incorporated into the widely-used MATLAB package MatCont, and similarly, the DDE predictor could be added to the well-known software DDE-BIFTOOL.

Since our method applies in all situations where the center manifold theorem holds, future research could focus on extending these techniques to a wider range of systems, including partial differential equations and stochastic models, thereby enhancing their versatility and potential for modelling complex real-world phenomena.

We conclude by noting that research on initiating the continuation of curves emanating from bifurcation points remains an active and dynamic area. In particular, starting the continuation of Shilnikov homoclinic bifurcation curves near zero-Hopf bifurcations remains a challenging and unresolved problem. Addressing this challenge will provide another powerful numerical tool for demonstrating the existence of chaos in dynamical systems, as was recently done in
[
6
]
, and will complement the research reported in
[
1
]
.

9.2
Derivation of the parameter-dependent coefficients for DDEs

Linear coefficients
K_{10}
,
K_{01}

To find the equations from which we can solve for
K_{10},K_{01}
, we follow the same steps as in the ODE case. Collecting the
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
terms in
Section

5
yields for
\mu=(10),(01)
the systems

\displaystyle-A^{\odot\star}jH_{00\mu}=J_{1}K_{\mu}r^{\odot\star}.

(146)

Write

K_{\mu}=\gamma_{1,\mu}e_{1}+\gamma_{2,\mu}e_{2},

(147)

for unknown
\gamma_{1,\mu},\gamma_{2,\mu}\in\mathbb{R}
and
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
the standard basis vectors. Substituting equation
Eq.

147
into
Eq.

146
and applying
Section

2.2.2
.1 results in

H_{00\mu}(\theta)=\gamma_{1,\mu}\Delta^{-1}(0)J_{1}e_{1}+\gamma_{2,\mu}\Delta(0)^{-1}J_{1}e_{2}.

(148)

The
\beta_{1}z
and
\beta_{2}z
terms yield the systems

\displaystyle(i\omega_{0}I_{n}-A^{\odot\star})jH_{10\mu}=[A_{1}(\varphi,K_{\mu})+B(\varphi,H_{00\mu})]r^{\odot\star}-(\delta_{\mu}^{10}+ib_{1,\mu})j\varphi,

(149)

where
\delta_{\mu}^{10}
is defined as in
Eq.

80
. Define
\Gamma_{i}(\varphi)=A_{1}(\varphi,e_{i})+B(\varphi,\Delta^{-1}(0)J_{1}e_{i})
.
Substituting equations
Eq.

147
and
Eq.

148
into equation
Eq.

149
then yields

\displaystyle(i\omega_{0}I_{n}-A^{\odot\star})jH_{10\mu}=[\gamma_{1,\mu}\Gamma_{1}(\varphi)+\gamma_{2,\mu}\Gamma_{2}(\varphi)]r^{\odot\star}-(\delta_{\mu}^{10}+ib_{1,\mu})j\varphi.

(150)

Applying the Fredholm solvability condition to equation
Eq.

150
results in

\delta_{\mu}^{10}+ib_{1,\mu}=p^{T}[\gamma_{1,\mu}\Gamma_{1}(\varphi)+\gamma_{2,\mu}\Gamma_{2}(\varphi)].

(151)

Taking the real and imaginary parts yield

\delta_{\mu}^{10}=\gamma_{1,\mu}\Re{[p^{T}\Gamma_{1}(\varphi)]}+\gamma_{2,\mu}\Re{[p^{T}\Gamma_{2}(\varphi)]},

(152)

and

b_{1,\mu}=\gamma_{1,\mu}\Im{[p^{T}\Gamma_{1}(\varphi)]}+\gamma_{2,\mu}\Im{[p^{T}\Gamma_{2}(\varphi)]}.

(153)

A solution
H_{10\mu}
of equation
Eq.

150
satisfying
\langle\varphi^{\odot},H_{10\mu}\rangle=0
can be obtained with
Section

2.2.2
:

\displaystyle H_{10\mu}(\theta)=B_{i\omega_{0}}^{INV}(\gamma_{1,01}\Gamma_{1}(\varphi)+\gamma_{2,01}\Gamma_{2}(\varphi),-(\delta_{\mu}^{10}+ib_{1,\mu})q)(\theta).

(154)

To free the unknown coefficients
\gamma_{1,\mu}
and
\gamma_{2,\mu}
we need to rewrite the equation as we did for ODEs. As with ODEs, applying the bordered inverse to the separate terms loses the original interpretation. Thus, we write
B^{Inv}_{i\omega_{0}}
whenever only the notation from
Section

2.2.2
is used, i.e.
v=B^{Inv}_{i\omega_{0}}(\eta,\xi_{0},\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{m})
is defined by equation
Eq.

38
from
Section

2.2.2
but does not solve
(i\omega_{0}I_{n}-A^{\odot\star})jv=jw
. With this we can rewrite (
133
) as

H_{10\mu}(\theta)=\gamma_{1,\mu}B^{Inv}_{i\omega_{0}}(\Gamma_{1}(\varphi))(\theta)+\gamma_{2,\mu}B^{Inv}_{i\omega_{0}}(\Gamma_{2}(\varphi))(\theta)-(\delta_{\mu}^{10}+ib_{1,\mu})B^{Inv}_{i\omega_{0}}(0,q)(\theta).

(155)

The important thing is that with all three terms combined,
Eq.

155
gives us the same expression as
Eq.

154
which solves equation
Eq.

150
. Collecting the
z^{2}\beta_{i}
and
z\bar{z}\beta_{i}
terms respectively yield the systems

\displaystyle(2i\omega_{0}I_{n}-A^{\odot\star})jH_{20\mu}

\displaystyle=[A_{1}(H_{2000},K_{\mu})+2B(\varphi,H_{10\mu})+B(H_{00\mu},H_{2000})

\displaystyle+B_{1}(\varphi,\varphi,K_{\mu})+C(\varphi,\varphi,H_{00\mu})]r^{\odot\star}-2(\delta_{\mu}^{10}+ib_{1,\mu})jH_{2000},

\displaystyle-A^{\odot\star}jH_{11\mu}

\displaystyle=[A_{1}(H_{1100},K_{\mu})+2\Re{\{B(\bar{\varphi},H_{10\mu})\}}+B(H_{00\mu},H_{1100})

\displaystyle+B_{1}(\varphi,\bar{\varphi},K_{\mu})+C(\varphi,\bar{\varphi},H_{00\mu})]r^{\odot\star}-2\delta_{\mu}^{10}jH_{1100}.

Both these systems are regular and their solutions follow from
Section

2.2.2
. The resulting equations are

\displaystyle H_{20\mu}(\theta)

\displaystyle=e^{2i\omega_{0}\theta}\Delta^{-1}(2i\omega_{0})[A_{1}(H_{2000},K_{\mu})+2B(\varphi,H_{10\mu})+B(H_{00\mu},H_{2000})

\displaystyle+B_{1}(\varphi,\varphi,K_{\mu})+C(\varphi,\varphi,H_{00\mu})]

\displaystyle-2(\delta_{\mu}^{10}+ib_{1,\mu})\Delta^{-1}(2i\omega_{0})[\Delta^{\prime}(2i\omega_{0})-\theta\Delta(2i\omega_{0})]H_{2000}(\theta),

(156)

\displaystyle H_{11\mu}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\Delta^{-1}(0)[A_{1}(H_{1100},K_{\mu})+2\Re{(B(\bar{\varphi},H_{10\mu}))}+B(H_{00\mu},H_{1100})

\displaystyle+B_{1}(\varphi,\bar{\varphi},K_{\mu})+C(\varphi,\bar{\varphi},H_{00\mu})]-2\delta_{\mu}^{10}\Delta^{-1}(0)[\Delta^{\prime}(0)-\theta\Delta(0)]H_{1100}(\theta),

We now substitute equations
Eq.

147
,
Eq.

148
and
Eq.

154
into the above expressions. For this, it is convenient to define for

the following functions

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{i}(u)+2B\left(v,B_{i\omega_{0}}^{Inv}(\Gamma_{i}(w))\right)+B_{1}(v,w,e_{i})+C(v,w,\Delta^{-1}(0)J_{1}e_{i}),

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{i}(u)+2\Re{\{B\left(v,B_{i\omega_{0}}^{Inv}(\Gamma_{i}(w))\right)\}}+B_{1}(v,w,e_{i})+C(v,w,\Delta^{-1}(0)J_{1}e_{i}).

Then the equations for
H_{20\mu}
and

\displaystyle=\gamma_{1,\mu}e^{2i\omega_{0}\theta}\Delta^{-1}(2i\omega_{0})\Lambda_{1}(H_{2000},\varphi,\varphi)+\gamma_{2,\mu}e^{2i\omega_{0}\theta}\Delta^{-1}(2i\omega_{0})\Lambda_{2}(H_{2000},\varphi,\varphi)

\displaystyle-2\delta_{\mu}^{10}\Delta^{-1}(2i\omega_{0})\left([\Delta^{\prime}(2i\omega_{0})-\theta\Delta(2i\omega_{0})]H_{2000}(\theta)+e^{2i\omega_{0}\theta}B\left(\varphi,B_{i\omega_{0}}^{Inv}(0,q)\right)\right),

\displaystyle-2ib_{1,\mu}\Delta^{-1}(2i\omega_{0})\left([\Delta^{\prime}(2i\omega_{0})-\theta\Delta(2i\omega_{0})]H_{2000}(\theta)+e^{2i\omega_{0}\theta}B\left(\varphi,B_{i\omega_{0}}^{Inv}(0,q)\right)\right),

\displaystyle H_{11\mu}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\gamma_{1,\mu}\Delta^{-1}(0)\Pi_{1}(H_{1100},\bar{\varphi},\varphi)+\gamma_{2,\mu}\Delta^{-1}(0)\Pi_{2}(H_{1100},\bar{\varphi},\varphi)

\displaystyle-2\delta_{\mu}^{10}\Delta^{-1}(0)\left([\Delta^{\prime}(0)-\theta\Delta(0)]H_{1100}(\theta)+\Re{}\left\{B\left(\bar{\varphi},B_{i\omega_{0}}^{Inv}(0,q)\right)\right\}\right)

\displaystyle-2b_{1,\mu}\Delta^{-1}(0)\Re{}\left\{iB\left(\bar{\varphi},B_{i\omega_{0}}^{Inv}(0,q)\right)\right\},

The
z^{2}\bar{z}\beta_{i}
terms yield the systems

\displaystyle(i\omega_{0}I_{n}-A^{\odot\star})jH_{21\mu}

\displaystyle=[A_{1}(H_{2100},K_{\mu})+2B(\varphi,H_{11\mu})+B(\bar{\varphi},H_{20\mu})+B(H_{00\mu},H_{2100})

\displaystyle+B(\overline{H}_{10\mu},H_{2000})+2B(H_{10\mu},H_{1100})+2B_{1}(\varphi,H_{1100},K_{\mu})

\displaystyle+B_{1}(\bar{\varphi},H_{2000},K_{\mu})+C(\varphi,\varphi,\overline{H}_{10\mu})+2C(\varphi,\bar{\varphi},H_{10\mu})

\displaystyle+2C(\varphi,H_{00\mu},H_{1100})+C(\bar{\varphi},H_{00\mu},H_{2000})+C_{1}(\varphi,\varphi,\bar{\varphi},K_{\mu})

\displaystyle+D(\varphi,\varphi,\bar{\varphi},H_{00\mu})]r^{\odot\star}-[2(\delta_{\mu}^{01}+ib_{2,\mu})j\varphi+(3\delta_{\mu}^{10}+ib_{1,\mu})jH_{2100}

\displaystyle+2c_{1}(0)jH_{10\mu}].

(158)

Applying the Fredholm Alternative to equation
Section

9.2
results in the equation

\displaystyle\delta_{\mu}^{01}+ib_{2,\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}p^{T}[A_{1}(H_{2100},K_{\mu})+2B(\varphi,H_{11\mu})+B(\bar{\varphi},H_{20\mu})+B(H_{00\mu},H_{2100})

\displaystyle+B(\overline{H}_{10\mu},H_{2000})+2B(H_{10\mu},H_{1100})+2B_{1}(\varphi,H_{1100},K_{\mu})+B_{1}(\bar{\varphi},H_{2000},K_{\mu})

\displaystyle+C(\varphi,\varphi,\overline{H}_{10\mu})+2C(\varphi,\bar{\varphi}.H_{10\mu})+2C(\varphi,H_{00\mu},H_{1100})+C(\bar{\varphi},H_{00\mu},H_{2000})

\displaystyle+C_{1}(\varphi,\varphi,\bar{\varphi},K_{\mu})+D(\varphi,\varphi,\bar{\varphi},H_{00\mu})],

where we used that

and

where

\displaystyle=\Re{[p^{T}\Gamma_{k}(\varphi)]},

(161)

\displaystyle P_{2k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Re{}\Bigl\{p^{T}\Bigl[\Gamma_{k}(H_{2100})+2B\left(\varphi,\Delta^{-1}(0)\Pi_{k}(H_{1100},\bar{\varphi},\varphi)\right)

\displaystyle+B\left(\bar{\varphi},e^{2i\omega_{0}\theta}\Delta^{-1}(2i\omega_{0})\Lambda_{k}(H_{2000},\varphi,\varphi)\right)+B\left(H_{2000},\overline{B_{i\omega_{0}}^{Inv}(\Gamma_{k}(\varphi))}\right)

\displaystyle+2B\left(H_{1100},B_{i\omega_{0}}^{Inv}(\Gamma_{k}(\varphi))\right)+2B_{1}(\varphi,H_{1100},e_{k})

\displaystyle+B_{1}(\bar{\varphi},H_{2000},e_{k})+C\left(\varphi,\varphi,\overline{B_{i\omega_{0}}^{Inv}(\Gamma_{k}(\varphi))}\right)+2C\left(\varphi,\bar{\varphi},B_{i\omega_{0}}^{Inv}(\Gamma_{k}(\varphi))\right)

\displaystyle+2C\left(\varphi,H_{1100},\Delta^{-1}(0)J_{1}e_{k}\right)+C(\bar{\varphi},H_{2000},\Delta^{-1}(0)J_{1}e_{k})+C_{1}(\varphi,\varphi,\bar{\varphi},e_{k})

\displaystyle+D(\varphi,\varphi,\bar{\varphi},\Delta^{-1}(0)J_{1}e_{k})+\Im{[p^{T}\Gamma_{k}(\varphi)]}\Bigl(-4B\left(\varphi,\Delta^{-1}(0)\Re{[iB\left(\bar{\varphi},B_{i\omega_{0}}^{Inv}(0,q)\right)]}\right)

\displaystyle-2iB\left(\bar{\varphi},e^{2i\omega_{0}\theta}\Delta^{-1}(2i\omega_{0})\left([\Delta^{\prime}(2i\omega_{0})-\theta\Delta(2i\omega_{0})]H_{2000}(0)+B\left(\varphi,B_{i\omega_{0}}^{Inv}(0,q)\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle\left.\left.\left.+iB\left(H_{2000},\overline{B_{i\omega_{0}}^{Inv}(0,q)}\right)-2iB\left(H_{1100},B_{i\omega_{0}}^{Inv}(0,q)\right)+iC\left(\varphi,\varphi,\overline{B_{i\omega_{0}}^{Inv}(0,q)}\right)\right.\right.\right.

\displaystyle-2iC\left(\varphi,\bar{\varphi},B_{i\omega_{0}}^{Inv}(0,q)\right)\Bigr)\Bigr]\Bigr\},

(162)

and

with

The coefficient

\displaystyle-A^{\odot\star}jH_{0003}

\displaystyle=[J_{1}K_{03}+M_{0003}]r^{\odot\star}.

10
Terms collected from the homological equation for ODEs

In this supplement, all the equations collected from the homological equation for the generalized Hopf bifurcation in ODEs
Eq.

52
are presented. The collected equations are obtained with the help of Mathematica. We use the truncated normal form expressed in terms of
\beta

\dot{w}=\lambda(\beta)w+c_{1}(\beta)w|w|^{2}+c_{2}(\beta)w|w|^{4}+c_{3}(0)w|w|^{6},\quad w\in\mathbb{C},\ \beta\in\mathbb{R}^{2},

where we expand

\displaystyle\lambda(\beta)

\displaystyle=i\omega_{0}+g_{1010}\beta_{1}+g_{1001}\beta_{2}+\frac{1}{2}g_{1020}\beta_{1}^{2}+g_{1011}\beta_{1}\beta_{2}+\frac{1}{2}g_{1002}\beta_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{6}g_{1003}\beta_{2}^{3},

\displaystyle c_{1}(\beta)

\displaystyle=c_{1}(0)+g_{2110}\beta_{1}+g_{2101}\beta_{2}+\frac{1}{2}g_{2120}\beta_{1}^{2}+g_{2111}\beta_{1}\beta_{2}+\frac{1}{2}g_{2102}\beta_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{6}g_{2103}\beta_{2}^{3},

\displaystyle c_{2}(\beta)

\displaystyle=c_{2}(0)+g_{3210}\beta_{1}+g_{3201}\beta_{2}.

Compared to
Section

4
we have that for
\mu=(10),(01)

\displaystyle g_{10\mu}

\displaystyle=\delta_{\mu}^{10}+b_{1,\mu}i,

\displaystyle g_{21\mu}

\displaystyle=\delta_{\mu}^{01}+b_{2,\mu}i,

For
\mu=(20),(02),(11)
we have
g_{10\mu}=b_{1,\mu}i
and
g_{21\mu}=b_{2,\mu}i
. The vectorfield
F
is expanded as
Section

4
. Note that
H_{ij\mu}=\overline{H}_{ji\mu}
.
